Management Appraisal Fiduciary Activities • Authority to make discretionary decisions and the matters in which independent judgment may be exercised should be defined expressly and communicated to supervisory personnel. Sufficient flexibility should be allowed to permit expedient action where it would be essential to the best interests of a fiduciary customer.
Recruitment of new personnel is an integral part of any management plan. The examiner can have greater confidence in the continuity of top and middle management if he or she knows that the bank has an inflow of new personnel at lower levels and that training procedures and advancement policies will keep the organization viable and dynamic.
Various types of training should be available in all institutions. The examiner should analyze the type and comprehensiveness of training offered and formulate an opinion about the quality of the program. Examples of training include: • "In-house" development programs.
The examiner should look at the variety of learning opportunities provided and the degree of participation by bank personnel. A bank must know if it offers the right training programs for its employees. Personnel evaluation will reveal whether a program is developing bank staff properly and provides a sound basis for promotions and salary increases.
Management Qualifications Although both the directors and management have important roles in formulating policies and establishing programs for the execution of functions, internal controls and audits. Management alone must implement such policies and programs within the organizational framework instituted by the board of directors. The exercise of fiduciary powers by a national bank should conform with the highest standards of professionalism.
Management of fiduciary activities should be judged on the basis of the ability to service its fiduciary accounts; both those presently under administration and those to which the bank has made future commitments.
Although a major measure of management's ability is the condition of fiduciary activities and the quality of fiduciary services offered, the effect that management will have on future fiduciary activities is highly significant. The depth of planning and the forward looking attitude of executive officers are relevant in assessing that impact.
Two questions should be posed: Is management competent to administer the bank's fiduciary business? Is management's competence manifested in its performance? The measurement of human qualities is always an elusive task at best and examiners must constantly remain open and innovative in their approach to management appraisal.
In assessing competence, the qualifications of management should be evaluated relative to duties and responsibilities assigned. For example, a fiduciary manager may have limited knowledge of fiduciary matters yet possess the managerial skills necessary to guide the affairs of fiduciary activities effectively. In such a case, the examiner should emphasize the need for fiduciary expertise in middle management levels. In small fiduciary departments, managers generally must be well-versed in all facets of fiduciary business engaged in by their respective banks. In banks where relationship banking is practiced, or the delivery of financial products and services has been integrated with the bank, managers of those activities must be particularly knowledgeable of sound fiduciary principles and practices.
Examiners should consider the following factors in evaluating management personnel. Information relative to those factors should be provided by the bank and retained in the working papers for future reference.
Education Training Experience Character What is the level of academic achievement and its relationship to the position held?
What is the level of professional training?
What schools or seminars has the person attended?
What is the nature of the person's fiduciary experience? Is such experience relevant to the person's duties and responsibilities?
The MIS should be as unique as the bank and/or fiduciary activities it serves. It should fit the philosophy of the bank and/or fiduciary department's long- and short-term goals, internal structures, budgets, and existing communications networks. The examiner should be aware of certain of the following fundamentals common to a successful MIS.
It addresses the kinds and frequency of decisions to be made, and by whom.
• It considers information needed and its availability.
It then focuses on relevant information and eliminates that which is not useful.
• It evaluates how ideas are communicated within the organization.
• It considers how and where decisions are implemented. It then provides information for the decisions that need to be made.
• It is kept simple. Too often an MIS becomes an end unto itself and ceases to be cost-justified.
• It produces current, accurate and relevant information.
In evaluating the MIS, the examiner should answer the following questions: Does it have the actual support of senior management of the bank and/or trust department?
• Are human considerations weighed independently when making decisions based on the MIS?
Is it flexible and can it allow for potential changes in growth, strategy, communications, and systems?
• Is it reviewed and evaluated annually by management?
Corporate Planning The increasing competition in the delivery of fiduciary products and services and the dynamic nature of the financial services industry demands ongoing planning.
The long-range plan for fiduciary activities is the joint responsibility of senior bank and fiduciary management and should be integrated with the long-range plan for the bank. Long-range planning is a continuous, systematic process of making risk-taking decisions that involve the future. The long-range plan should include the method for its execution and ways of measuring results against expectations. There are three common misconceptions of long-range planning: it is misunderstood to be a means of forecasting the future, dealing with future decisions, or eliminating risks. It is none of these.
Instead, long-range planning is necessary merely because the future cannot be forecast; long-range planning consists of making current decisions involving the future. The taking of risks cannot be eliminated, but the risks taken must be the right ones.
The long-range plan provides the focus for fiduciary activities to function successfully. The plan includes its goals, directions, and expectations and alerts management to the decisions, commitments, and efforts of fiduciary personnel.
Much can be written about the merits of the "top down" versus the "bottom up" approach to long-range planning. In general, a balance between the two methods Management Appraisal Fiduciary Activities often results from the prevailing circumstances in the trust department. Regardless of the predominance of one method over the other, the planning process should involve the participation of management at all levels.
Through their participation, managers and supervisors can become more creative and gain a more comprehensive understanding of the challenges confronting fiduciaries.
Until recently, contingency planning was relatively uncommon in banking. However, the rapid and dramatic changes occurring in the financial services industry have made it imperative that the long-range plan allow for the occurrence of various contingencies. The contingency plan permits management to adjust quickly or exploit opportunities when possible events become reality.
The following should be considered when evaluating the fiduciary activities long-range planning process: • Is long-range planning a continuous, ongoing process that improves responsiveness to internal and external changes?
• Is the long-range plan integrated and compatible with the long-range plan for the bank?
• Does the long-range plan include programs for its implementation and are they directed effectively?
• Are there provisions in the long-range plan for contingencies?
• Is the long-range planning process cost effective?
• Does management monitor the long-range plan by reviewing feedback information to measure results?
Has the long-range plan, or portions thereof, been disseminated to all managers and supervisors, that have a need to know?
Financial Performance A significant measure of management's effectiveness is the financial performance of fiduciary activities. The examiner should review that performance with management. The review should include the quality and trend of earnings, management and environmental factors, and the prospects for future earnings. The performance of fiduciary activities, as perceived by management, should be weighed against the results of that review.
Developments in the fiduciary industry have emphasized the need to standardize approaches to trust profit accounting. "Measuring Trust Profitability," published by the ABA in October 1979, developed the following five guidelines for a trust profit accounting plan.
• Fiduciary income should be defined as though the department were an independent organization.
Marketing Expansion of the financial services industry presents new opportunities for banks and, particularly, fiduciary activities. With greater emphasis placed on banks to increase fee income, the financial expertise found in fiduciary activities is being called upon. The deregulated environment in which banks compete places considerable emphasis on the introduction of new financial products and services. The ability to compete successfully in those markets can affect a bank's future significantly.
Product superiority can provide a competitive advantage which should be capitalized upon. Banks must offer products which are competitive with those in the marketplace. At the same time, banks must be able to respond rapidly to changes in regulations, competition, and consumer interests. Customers of financial products and services are becoming increasingly sophisticated and capable of making sound value judgments concerning such products and services. Often, the customer of financial products and services is influenced more by their quality and value than by institutional loyalty to their sponsors. As marketing philosophy shifts from a product orientation to a broad range of financial products and services, marketing efforts must become more entrepreneurial.
The initial requirement of any successful marketing approach is the full support of bank and fiduciary activities management. Most banks lack the financial means and geographic location to serve all potential markets. Because of these limitations, market research must identify consumer needs and determine which market segments are compatible with bank capabilities. Caution must be exercised to work within the limits of managerial and financial resources as well as to assess competitive advantages. Market segmenting involves identifying potentially profitable customer groups and deciding where emphasis should be concentrated. In addition to determining key market segments, delivery systems must be established to serve them.
Meeting consumer needs involves providing current products and services to new customers or new services to existing customers. New products and services should be market-tested beforehand and a cost effective means of delivery determined. Pricing is usually a combination of three variables: cost, customer's willingness to pay, and linkage to other products and services of the bank. When financial products and services are integrated with the bank through relationship banking, a cooperative marketing approach is essential.
Meetings with Directors, Committees and Management When an integrated bank examination is performed, the examiner assigned to review fiduciary activities should coordinate with the examiner-in-charge all meetings.
and reviews with directors, committees, and management.
Directors may assign certain fiduciary functions to fiduciary committees. Reports prepared for committees, along with minutes of committee meetings, indicate the involvement and interest of committee members in their assigned duties and responsibilities. When circumstances warrant, the examiner should attend fiduciary committee meetings held during the examination. By attending meetings, the examiner can also inform committee members of OCC policies and discuss matters relevant to the trust department. The objectives in attending committee meetings are: • To observe the methods and practices employed by the committee in executing their duties.
• To assess the extent of participation of committee members.
• To evaluate the committee's thoroughness in performing their assigned duties and responsibilities.
To disclose weaknesses or deficiencies and to make recommendations for correction.
• To solicit the committee's views on the operation of fiduciary activities and any proposed changes in policy that might effect future operations.
The examiner may discuss such matters with the trust audit committee. This might be preferable when resistance of senior management has been encountered in effecting corrective action for exceptions noted during the examination, or when audit follow up to exceptions is necessary.
The examiner assigned responsibility for the fiduciary portion of an activity should attend the meeting with the board of directors when the Uniform Interagency Trust Rating System (UITRS) composite rating of the trust department is 4 or 5.
• Accommodate new financial products and services, and future growth.
Accounting and Control Fiduciary operations is responsible for the maintenance and proper accounting of essential trust records. Any system of accounting control, whether it be handposted, machine-posted, or an automated EDP system, should be accurate, subject to proper controls, produce timely information, and be flexible enough to accommodate anticipated growth.
With the advent of minicomputer based fiduciary accounting systems, most banks have installed some form of automated accounting system. Those systems can be purchased outright and operated "in-house" by the bank or may be purchased from an outside vendor or service bureau that provides remote processing capability. The bank also may contract for fiduciary recordkeeping services from other affiliated or nonaffiliated banks. All contracted recordkeeping services must be subject to a written agreement that contains specific information relative to services furnished to the bank and the type and frequency of reports provided.
Despite the wide variety of systems used, each should be capable of providing the basic accounting records outlined in Precedent and Opinion 9.2020, Minimum Standards of Information (MSI) for Trust EDP Systems.
The examiner should use those standards as a guideline in evaluating the particular accounting system and management reports being used by the bank. The ability of the system to generate records in accordance with the MIS is necessary both to facilitate the examination and to provide fundamental information for the preparation of necessary management reports, such as: • Investment analysis reviews.
The basic controls that underlie any trust accounting system are summarized in Precedent and Opinion 9.2000. The focal point of asset control is the general ledger which provides the master control for all fiduciary assets and liabilities. Fiduciary assets consist of all of the property, at carrying value, being held in individual fiduciary accounts. For fiduciary accounting purposes, liabilities are defined as the carrying value of all individual accounts. Assets should always equal liabilities and control is achieved by balancing individual account ledgers against the general ledger. The fiduciary accounting formula may be contrasted to the standard balance sheet accounting formula: assets equal liabilities plus capital. Depending on the size of fiduciary activities and sophistication of the accounting system, the general ledger may be capable of grouping assets by security type and accounts within specific categories, such as estates or living trusts.
System Reconcilement and Control To ensure the continued accuracy of fiduciary records, fiduciary accounting systems must be subject to routine reconcilement and control procedures. Examiners should review procedures to ascertain that reconcilements are performed properly on a timely basis and provide for an independent proof of input processing.
Any reconcilement items to the daily settlement should be adequately documented, and cleared on a timely basis.
Access to fiduciary accounting records should be properly controlled. Precedent and Opinion 9.2021 requires that banks utilizing automated EDP systems should provide for terminal security through the establishment of password control procedures.
Separation of Principal and Income A fundamental and distinguishing principle in fiduciary accounting as opposed to other bank accounting is the requirement that separate records be maintained for principal and income of a trust. That requirement is most Operations Fiduciary Activities evident in the allocation of principal and income cash.
The distinction between principal and income cash is necessary to account for separate beneficial interests which are present in most personal trust accounts. A trust may provide that one or several persons are entitled to receive current income of a trust. Those beneficiaries are referred to as income beneficiaries.
Income is defined as the return derived from the use of principal and may consist of dividends, interests, rents or royalties. Principal beneficiaries or remaindermen are entitled to the property or corpus of the trust and any appreciation derived from the holding of such property.
The trust instrument may provide specific guidance for allocation of principal and income. Additional guidance is provided under the revised Uniform Principal and Income Act adopted by most states. Any bank system of recordkeeping must provide for separate accounting of principal and income. Fiduciary personnel responsible for originating or verifying transactions should also be knowledgeable regarding the requirement for allocating principal and income applicable to each account class as well as the type of receipt and disbursement.
Trust Cash Cash accounts held represent uninvested cash held by individual trust accounts. This cash is generally held, pending investment or distribution, in a single demand deposit account with the bank.
The trust checking account should be subject to periodic reconciliation. Fiduciary activities utilizing automated accounting systems must reconcile cash records to the DDA account statement(s) as of each posting date. To facilitate the daily verification of fiduciary cash, the fiduciary may utilize a separate fiduciary DDA disbursement account. This disbursement account is essentially a zero balance account used to control and segregate outstanding checks.
Uninvested Cash As a practical matter, the use of time open accounts by fiduciary departments should be largely eliminated in favor of the active investment of cash balances. 12 CFR 9.10 requires that banks shall adopt and follow written procedures intended to ensure that funds awaiting permanent investment or distribution are invested within reasonable time frames at the maximum rate of return available for trust-quality short-term investments. Both income cash and principal cash should be made productive at the earliest possible opportunity and at the maximum prudent rate available.
The application of that standard must be based necessarily upon a review of the circumstances relevant to each department. To ensure compliance with 12 CFR 9.10(a), many banks use automated accounting systems to provide cash management or "sweep" programs. Those programs automatically invest cash balances in selected short-term investment vehicles. Cash management programs are usually flexible and allow the bank to use a wide range of investment options. The decision to leave any cash uninvested must be properly supported and documented in the bank's records.
Examiners should review investment options used by the bank to ensure that they are reasonable considering the circumstances of each account involved. In the final analysis, responsibility for adherence to the fiduciary duty of making trust property productive rests with the bank and its board of directors. Banks may elect to charge an additional fee for providing cash management services if such fees are specifically authorized by local law, the terms of the governing instrument, or consent of all beneficiaries. In addition, the bank must disclose fully such fee as a separate line item on customer account statements.
Cash Overdrafts Examiners should ascertain the bank's policy for individual account overdrafts. Account overdrafts can result from a variety of causes, including timing differences associated with investment transaction activity or cash distributions. Examiners should ensure that all overdrafts are temporary in nature, made only for proper purposes, and appropriately reflected on account records. Fiduciary departments should minimize overdrafts by adopting administrative policies which consider the liquidity requirements of individual accounts.
A net cash overdraft is a loan from the trustee bank to the account and does not violate 12 CFR 9.12(e) concerning inter-account loans.
Pledge Requirements Regulation 12 CFR 9.10(b) requires that funds held-intrust and funds of discretionary accounts on deposit in the bank awaiting investment or distribution, which are in excess of FDIC coverage, must be fully collateralized by direct obligations of the U.S. government or other specified readily marketable securities. That pledge requirement would extend to all deposits with the commercial bank, including demand deposit accounts, certificates of deposit, bank money market deposit accounts, and savings accounts. The bank should adopt and implement procedures which require periodic review of the adequacy of collateral pledged in accordance with 12 CFR 9.10(b).
The proper amount to collateralize for a single fiduciary account with one beneficiary can be calculated by adding the funds described as follows: • The account's net demand deposit account balance (as shown on the trust department records).
• Outstanding checks drawn on the account's demand deposit account.
• Funds deposited in interest-bearing accounts awaiting investment or distribution, as distinguished from invested funds.
• Funds held in suspense and operating accounts, which can be identified as property of the account.

Operations Fiduciary Activities Individual account totals can then be added to determine the minimum necessary collateral for a particular day. Using this formula, an overdraft representing a single account's net cash position should not be part of the calculation for the department total. If other formulas that are not performed at the individual account level are used, net overdrafts must be added.
Securities Income Processing The securities processing unit is responsible for the collection of dividends and interest payable on assets held in trust accounts. Depending upon the type of asset, income may be based on regular or fixed payments, such as bond interest, or may be received on an irregular basis, such as in the case of mineral interests, royalties, or farm income. The bank should establish appropriate tickler systems to monitor the timely receipt of all asset income. In smaller departments, tickler systems may consist of a card index which details fixed income payments. In departments utilizing automated recordkeeping systems, information on interest income is input directly to the system when the asset is established on account records. The bank may also subscribe to one of several daily pricing services which provide automated updates to asset records through tape entry of dividend and capital change information.
Through those automated updates, most systems are able to generate reports of anticipated dividend or interest payments referred to as dividend and interest maps. Those reports provide a breakdown of individual accounts holding a specific asset and the amount of the dividend or interest payment due. Once the dividend or interest payment is received by the trust department and reconciled to the anticipated amounts shown on the dividend and interest maps, the system will generate entries to automatically credit accounts with the income due.
Dividend/Income Claims Procedures Over or underpayments of dividends or interest received are usually posted to a dividend and interest suspense account pending further research. In the case of overpayments, funds are held pending a documented claim from the rightful owner. In the absence of a claim, the trust department is required to comply with state laws regarding escheat or disposition of unclaimed funds. When the bank has been underpaid or has not received the full income due, personnel should follow established procedures to determine the reason for underpayment and file a written claim against the appropriate party. Examiners should determine that the bank has detailed records of dividend and interest claim activity as well as a list of aged dividends and interest receivable.
Disbursement of Trust Assets Trust operations is responsible for the control and processing of all trust disbursements. Disbursements may be in the form of cash, securities, or other assets.
All requests for disbursements of trust cash should be in writing, indicating whether the disbursement is to be from principal or income cash, and approved by authorized personnel. Distribution of securities is somewhat more complicated because the bank is usually required to have the securities registered in the name of the distributee prior to disbursement. Examiners should ensure that the bank maintains full documentation, including receipts, to support the proper distribution of trust assets.
Nominee Registration Most trust agreements authorize the bank to register securities in nominee form; also, the vast majority of jurisdictions have made statutory provision for the use of nominee registration. For the trust department to utilize nominee registration, the board of directors must authorize the execution of a partnership agreement between designated officers or employees of the bank.
The partnership agreement establishes a legal name which should be registered with the state in which the bank is located and with the American Society of Corporate Secretaries. Securities held in the trust department can then be registered in the name of that nominee. In addition to simplifying the transfer of stock, the use of nominee registration facilitates the collection of dividends and interest when the bank receives a single dividend or interest check for each nominee.
name in which a security is registered. Otherwise, the trust department would receive a separate dividend or interest check for each individual form in which an applicable security is registered. Examiners should ascertain that the bank's nominee partnership agreements are reviewed periodically to ensure that they are current. Prior to utilizing nominee registration, banks should consult local law regarding its use.
Custody of Assets Trust operations is responsible for safeguarding fiduciary assets. 12 CFR 9.13 requires that assets of each account should be kept separate from the assets of the bank and maintained under joint control. To the extent permitted by law, trust departments may hold fiduciary assets off-premise, as long as the bank maintains adequate records to identify them as assets of relevant accounts.
On-Premises Custody When the bank maintains custody of securities on premises, examiners should review the bank's system of internal controls, security precautions, and operational procedures to ensure that trust assets are properly safeguarded against possible loss. Basic internal controls that should be followed in all departments, regardless of size, include: • Dual Control-As required by 12 CFR 9.13, at least two authorized persons should be present when handling or having access to securities. Written procedures should be developed by the department requiring that dual control procedures be Operations Fiduciary Activities followed in all securities processing and control functions.
• Access to the Trust Vault and Securities Processing Areas-Access should be restricted to designated authorized personnel. Persons accessing the vault should sign vault logs or other control documentation. Persons controlling the vault should verify that such personnel are authorized to enter the vault.
Separation of Duties - Persons assigned responsibility to originate entries or trust vault instructions should not be authorized to access the vault or securities cage. Job responsibilities should reflect the need to separate control functions to minimize the possibility of defalcation.
Physical Vault Protection Measures The vault used for the safekeeping of trust assets should provide for minimum security devices consistent with the requirements of 12 CFR 21, Minimum Security Devices and Procedures. That regulation requires the installation of appropriate lighting, alarm, and other physical security controls.
Security movement and control records should detail all asset movements, deposits, and withdrawals, including temporary withdrawals, and should include: • The initials of the joint custodians.
Appropriate notations on the source of assets deposited and purposes for which assets are withdrawn.
Off-Premises Custody Increasingly, banks have chosen to hold fiduciary assets in an off-premise depository. Depending upon the type of asset and the size of the department, assets may be held at a national or regional depository, with the Federal Reserve, or at a correspondent bank. Fiduciaries may also choose to become indirect participants in a depository. Under this arrangement, referred to as a "piggy back," a correspondent relationship exists in which one bank may assign custody of securities and trade clearance authority to another bank which is a direct depository participant. In either case, the fiduciary should ensure that adequate controls and safeguards have been established by the depository and that the depository provides adequate insurance coverage against possible loss or theft. As previously noted under Asset Records, the accounting system should record the actual location of each asset.
Examiners should ascertain that the bank has implemented procedures for the daily reconciliation of changes in the depository position, as well as a full reconciliation at least monthly. Any exceptions should be thoroughly investigated. Examiners should also ensure that the bank has established appropriate dual control procedures for the release of securities from the depository. Whenever the release of securities is directed via telephone or other terminal system, the trust department should require that such instruction requests be properly confirmed in writing and verified.
Terminal interfaces used to effect depository withdrawals should be subject to appropriate password access controls.
Securities Trade Processing Fiduciary operations or securities processing is responsible for the processing of securities trading and settlement activities involving: • Receipt of trade authorization and written trade instruction.
• Placement of the trade with the appropriate broker for execution.
• Reconcilement of trade information to broker confirmations.
• Receipt or delivery of securities against payment.
Trade Orders Trade orders for individual accounts can be received from several sources depending on the bank's investment responsibility. Trade orders can be received from the bank's authorized investment personnel, from custcmers or from outside investment advisors. 12 CFR 12 establishes minimum recordkeeping and notification requirements for securities transactions. Section 12.3 of that regulation requires that trade orders and associated trade information be documented on separate order tickets or trade memorandum and include: • The account for which the order was executed.
• Whether the transaction is subject to any special trading instructions.
• The time the order was received by the trader as well as the time the order was placed with the broker/dealer.
• The unit and aggregate price at which the order was executed.
Those records should be maintained by the bank in a manner which provides a chronological file of all daily trade activity. In many cases, trade tickets are controlled by prenumbered multipart forms which are utilized to monitor an individual trade from initiation to delivery and posting to account records.

Operations Fiduciary Activities Trade Executions A trade is executed when an agreement is reached to purchase or sell a security on a given date at a given price. That information is communicated to the bank by the broker who handled the trade. Under 12 CFR 12, the bank is required to continuously monitor execution capabilities of broker/dealers utilized to effect trust department trades. That evaluation should consider: • Timeliness and accuracy of trade execution and settlement services provided by the broker.
• The broker's reputation and level of experience.
• Quality of research services provided by the broker/dealer.
Trade Confirmation Following notification of execution, the trade is confirmed in writing to the bank by the broker/dealer. The confirmation should include the full details of the trade and be reconciled to the original trade memoranda by parties independent of trade placement activities. Under 12 CFR 12, Section 12.4, the bank is required to send a copy of the confirmation or similar notification to the customer, detailing the transaction and including all remuneration received in the following situations: • For accounts in which the bank exercises no investment responsibility, such notifications must be sent to the customer within five business days of receipt by the bank. The customer may agree in writing to waive notification. However, the waiver should specifically acknowledge the customer's right to receive that information.
• For accounts in which the bank exercises investment discretion in other than an agency capacity, the bank is required to disclose specific trade information only when requested by authorized power holders. In that case, the bank may charge a reasonable fee for providing this service.
• For accounts in which the bank exercises discretionary authority in a managing agency capacity, the bank can disclose trade activity through quarterly itemized statements of account holdings which should include a complete listing of transactions.
The bank is required to provide additional trade information if requested by the customer and may charge an additional fee for this service.
• For accounts which are designated as periodic investment plans, the bank is required to furnish as promptly as possible to the customer a statement of the account showing the funds and securities in the custody of the bank. This statement should provide detail of debits and credits resulting from trade activity as well as any service charges or commissions paid by the customer. The bank is required to provide additional information if requested by the customer and may charge an additional fee for this service.
Trade Settlement The settlement of a trade occurs when securities are exchanged for cash payment. The date of the settlement is contracted upon execution of the trade by the broker/dealer. The settlement date is generally set at five business days after the execution of the trade, but may vary depending on the type of security being traded. In some instances, the actual receipt of the security may exceed the contracted settlement date.
Banks should establish specific written procedures for monitoring traded positions which remain open past settlement date.
Trade Posting There are two types of accounting methods utilized by banks in posting securities trades. The methods are referred to as contractual settlement date accounting and actual settlement date accounting. Under the contractual settlement date accounting method, trades are posted to customer's accounts on the contractual settlement date specified on the trade confirmation, whether or not the security is actually received. An offsetting cash entry for payment of the trade is made to a separate open purchases and sales suspense account. This entry is cleared when the securities are received and payment is made. Actual settlement date accounting involves the posting of trades to customer accounts on actual date of settlement rather than on the contractual settlement date. Many automated trust accounting systems enter pending trade notifications to customers' accounts when trades are executed.
Offsetting cash entries are not made until actual settlement occurs. Those systems can also monitor open trades and produce daily management reports listing all open trades past contractual settlement dates.
Institutional Delivery System - Securities Settlement With the dramatic increase in securities trading volume and settlement activities, a need for a less complex and more accurate national securities settlement and delivery system became necessary. The SEC and several securities exchanges adopted rules which encouraged banks to utilize the institutional securities settlement services offered by regional and national depositories.
Those depository services provide a nationwide electronic trading network which offers more efficient and timely trade settlements. They also reduce the incidence of failed or rejected trades and further the goal of securities immobilization by effecting transfers through established book-entry systems.
The Securities and Exchange Commission and New York Stock Exchange Rule 387, generally mandate the use of the Institutional Delivery System where the bank and broker are either direct or indirect depository paroperations Fiduciary Activities ticipants. Those rules require that depository participants who do not utilize Institutional Delivery Systems can no longer settle trades on a delivery versus payment, or C.O.D., basis, but must pay for trades on contractual or theoretical settlement date regardless of whether the security is actually received. Because of continuing changes in securities processing and trade settlement systems, it is likely that most trust departments will consider depository participation either through a direct or "piggy back" arrangement.
Securities Lending Many bank fiduciary departments are becoming active in the lending of securities from custody, agency, safekeeping and fiduciary accounts. Fiduciary departments, insurance companies, and investment advisory firms are major lenders of securities. Securities dealers are usually the borrowers. U.S. government securities make up the largest component of securities lending activities. To a lesser extent, the bank may be lending corporate stocks and bonds. Prior to initiating any formal securities lending program, the bank should develop and implement written policies, procedures and controls. Those procedures should be designed to enable the bank to conform with applicable laws, regulations and rulings, and minimize the potential risks associated with securities lending.
Review of the Borrower's Creditworthiness As in all lending arrangements, the fiduciary should conduct a detailed analysis and review of the borrower by persons qualified to make credit decisions. That review should include, at a minimum, procedures for a complete analysis of the borrower's financial statements as well as a review of current management and any other material change bearing on the creditworthiness of the borrower. The fiduciary should be fully aware of the financial strength and management capabilities of those parties with whom securities lending activities are transacted. Information on the borrower should be kept current and subject to periodic review.
In addition, lending concentrations with any one borrower should be avoided.
Formal Lending Agreement All securities lending arrangements should be entered into pursuant to a detailed written agreement outlining the duties and responsibilities of the participants. The agreement should disclose all facts relevant to the securities lending transaction, including: • The types and minimum amounts of collateral coverage being maintained.
• Custody of collateral and procedures to maintain adequate margin levels.
• Procedures for collecting interest and dividend payments on securities loaned.
Examiners should ensure that the bank has reviewed the agreements of all accounts participating in securities lending programs to ensure that the governing documents authorize such activity.
Types of Collateral Three of the most common forms of collateral used in securities lending programs are cash, securities, and letters of credit.
Cash Collateral When cash is accepted as collateral for securities loaned, it is usually invested in money market instruments. The fiduciary department may invest cash collateral in own bank liabilities or those of its affiliates.
Investments may include repurchase agreements, foreign time deposits, commercial paper, Eurodollar Cds, and variable amount or master note arrangements.
Income received from the invested collateral is often the source of compensation for those parties involved in securities lending arrangements. The income may be divided among the borrower, the account lending the securities and the bank, in accordance with a negotiated agreement.
Securities Collateral When securities are used to collateralize security lending arrangements, the trustee must obtain appropriate pledge and custody of the collateral. Registered securities, serving as collateral on extended term (30 days or more) securities lending transactions, should be registered in the name of the bank or in the name of the bank's nominee. The percentage of advances on collateral margin or the extent of discount from the market value should be determined by: • The type of security pledged.
Letters of Credit Letters of credit can be used to collateralize securities loans. Letters of credit used for such a purpose should be obtained from a third party and should be irrevocable.
Maintenance of Adequate Collateral Margins The bank's policies for collateral margin requirements should be specifically detailed in the security lending agreement. Minimum collateral requirements should be evaluated for adequacy in light of: • The type and character of the security pledged.
• The historic and anticipated price volatility of the security.

Operations Fiduciary Activities • The duration of the securities lending agreement.
The creditworthiness of the borrower.
Minimum collateral requirements should specifically address the need to provide adequate coverage for income accrued on securities subject to lending arrangements. In addition, securities lending involving assets of employee benefit plans are subject to minimum collateral requirements established by the Department of Labor of 102 percent of market value.
Bank Secrecy Act The Currency and Foreign Transactions Reporting Act (known as the Bank Secrecy Act) was enacted October 26, 1970, to discourage the use of currency in illegal transactions and to identify unusual or questionable transactions that could aid in criminal, tax and other regulatory investigations. The regulations promulgated to accomplish these objectives (31 CFR 103) contain extensive reporting and recordkeeping requirements.
The definitional provisions of the act include bank trust departments within its requirements. The act defines "person" as "An individual, a corporation, a partnership, a trust or estate, a joint stock company, an association, a syndicate, joint venture, or other unincorporated organization or group, and all entities cognizable as legal personalities" (31 CFR 103. The examiner may rely upon and should not duplicate procedures satisfactorily performed as a part of internal or external audit functions.
Internal Audit Traditionally, the primary objective of the audit function has been the detection of errors and irregularities and the determination of compliance with a bank's policies and controls. Auditors have been properly concerned with ascertaining whether the policies and procedures used for the administration of accounts, and the processing and recording of transactions comply with policies prescribed by the board of directors and senior management. As part of their audit program, internal auditors perform tests and procedures that enable them to reach such determinations.
The responsibilities of internal auditors, however, have been expanded to include the appraisal of the soundness and adequacy of accounting, operating, and administrative controls. In addition, internal auditors may review, where appropriate, the formulation and revision of policies and procedures. Audit participation may detect weaknesses in safeguards, controls, and audit trails, allowing management to correct such problems during the planning and implementation phases.
The OCC believes that examiners should evaluate the audit program based on the general criteria discussed in this section. The major factors that must be considered by the examiner in reviewing and evaluating the internal audit function are the competence and the independence of the internal auditors and the adequacy and the effectiveness of the audit program.
Competence of Internal Auditors The responsibilities and qualifications of internal auditors may vary, depending upon the size and complexity of a bank's fiduciary activities and on the emphasis placed on the audit function by the directorate and management. 12 CFR 9.9 states that the internal auditors should be responsible only to the board of directors of the directors' audit committee.
In large banks, the general auditor often is a manager who fulfills his responsibilities through the work of other people. In smaller banks, the responsibilities for internal audit may be placed with an officer or employee designated as a part-time auditor. In other instances, several employees may divide the audit responsibility. The qualifications discussed below should not be viewed as minimum requirements; rather, they are intended to be matters the examiner must consider in evaluating the work performed by the internal auditor or audit department.
The fiduciary audit manager should possess the following qualifications: A college degree with a business major or comparable business experience.
• Commitment to a program of continuing education and professional development.
Fiduciary audit experience, organizational and technical skills commensurate with the responsibilities assigned.
• Ability to communicate, both orally and in writing.
The fiduciary auditor must possess the proper education and training to fully understand trust administrative and operational functions. College courses are one source. Adequate education may be achieved, however, through participation in courses sponsored by industry groups or in-house training programs. Significant work experience in the fiduciary and investment department of a bank also would support the adequate training requirement. Professional qualifications are evidenced by certification as a Chartered Bank Auditor, Certified Internal Auditor or Certified Public Accountant.
In addition to prior education, the auditor should be committed to a program of continuing education by attending technical meetings and seminars and reviewing current literature on trust auditing and banking. The auditor's organizational skills should be reflected in the comprehensiveness and adequacy of the audit program. Technical skills are demonstrated through the use of appropriate audit techniques, such as completion of internal control and other questionnaires, testing transactions (selected by statistical or other sampling methods) flowcharting, and computer assisted auditing procedures.
In considering the qualifications of the audit staff, the examiner should review the bank's practices for hiring and training internal auditors. Educational and experience qualifications required in hiring should be established for each position in the audit department. A training curriculum should provide for the professional progression of the audit staff. Opportunities for advancement should be afforded personnel to promote continuity of audit supervision.
Indicators of the competence of the internal auditor include the quality of the work performed and the ability to communicate the results. The examiner's conclusions about the auditor's competence should encompass the adequacy of the audit program, training, and the quality of reports and reviews.

Internal and External Audits Fiduciary Activities Independence of Internal Auditors The ability of the internal audit function to achieve its objectives depends, in large part, on the independence maintained by audit personnel. Frequently, an indication of the auditor's independence may be obtained by determining to whom or to what level within the organization the auditor reports administratively and to whom the auditor reports the results of audit work performed.
The internal audit function should be directed by the board of directors or the trust audit committee. This arrangement enables the audit function to be independent of bank management and assists the directors in fulfilling their responsibilities under 12 CFR 9.
The auditor should be given the authority necessary to perform a satisfactory audit. The auditor's authority should include access to all records deemed necessary for the proper conduct of the audit.
Internal auditors must maintain independence in appearance as well as in fact. They should be instructed about the standards required for their individual behavior and the performance of their field work.
The bank's audit department must be independent of all functions that it evaluates. Independence permits the auditor to perform an objective evaluation and provide suggestions for improvement. For instance, to ensure an objective evaluation of the bank's security arrangements, the auditor should not also serve as security officer of the bank. Similarly, auditors should not authorize changes in systems without the specific concurrence of management, nor should they make ledger entries, sign checks or make investment decisions.
Adequacy of Internal Audit Program Factors that should be considered in determining the adequacy of the audit program include: • Scope and frequency of the audit work.
The scope of the internal audit program must be sufficient to accomplish audit objectives. Audit procedures set forth in the Audit Function Questionnaire must be performed.
National banks must perform audit procedures based on an evaluation of risk and prepare and maintain written documentation outlining the design, application and coverage of risk measurement plans. A well-developed plan should include: 1. Relevant factors to evaluate risk associated with each area of audit interest. Such factors include: • Dollar value and liquidity of assets.
• Volume, complexity, nature of activity, and projection of future growth.
• Effectiveness of administrative and accounting controls.
• Past performance, condition, and stability of the operation.
Systems.
Supervision and personnel competence.
• The potential materiality and frequency of errors or irregularities associated with specific activities and a history of operating charge offs and other losses.
2. Evaluation of risk in all significant fiduciary activities which are not subject to OCC mandatory audit procedures. An evaluation of risk must be used to determine additional audit procedures necessary in areas subject to OCC mandatory audit procedures.
3. Determination of appropriate audit procedures and frequency of application based on the degree of risk, audit management's professional judgment and other relevant factors.
For the examiner to have a sound basis to evaluate the adequacy of the internal audit program, there must exist a documented record of the work performed. Audit working papers provide a basis for the examiner's review of the internal audit function. They indicate the depth and coverage of the auditor's work. When evaluating the working papers of the internal auditors, several factors should be considered. The content of the working papers is one indicator of an auditor's competence and adherence to professional standards. Working papers should be legible, orderly, and properly indexed so that supportive documentation can be located easily. They should substantiate the procedures followed, tests performed, information obtained, and conclusions reached in the audit report.
Although audit work programs are an integral part of the working papers, they are sufficiently important to deserve separate attention. They serve as primary evidence of the audit procedures performed and, as such, they should be written for all areas of fiduciary activity.
Each program should provide a clear and concise description of the audit work required. Individual procedures should be presented in a logical sequence. The detailed procedures included in the program will vary, depending upon the nature, size and complexity of the bank's fiduciary activities.
Completion of the specific procedures included in all work programs should enable the internal auditor to reach appropriate conclusions that will satisfy audit objectives. Audit reports must be consistent with conclusions. In addition, they should include, where appropriate, recommendations by the internal auditor for any required corrective action.

Internal and External Audits Fiduciary Activities Effectiveness of Internal Audit Program The effectiveness of an internal audit program depends largely on the: • Adequacy of planning.
Auditor competence.
Other factors to consider in determining the effectiveness of the audit program would be similar to those addressed in the Examination Planning and Control section.
The auditor must communicate all findings and recommendations in a clear, concise manner, identifying problems and suggesting solutions, and submit reports as soon as practicable after completion of the related work. Reports should be routed to those officials who have both the responsibility and authority to implement the suggested changes. Prompt and effective management response to the auditor's recommendations is essential to the effectiveness of the audit program. The general auditor should determine that recommendations have been implemented and, if not, determine the reason for lack of implementation. Periodic reports and discussions, summarizing audit activity and results, should be provided to the trust audit committee and senior management.
Internal Audit Review The examiner's review and evaluation of the internal audit function should be performed during planning or upon commencement of the activity (see Planning and Control section).
It is a key element in determining the activity scope. This review should include an evaluation of the competence and independence of the internal auditors to enable the examiner to conclude whether the work performed by them is acceptable, partially acceptable, or not acceptable. Often, it is more efficient for the examiner to determine the competence and independence of the internal auditor before addressing the adequacy and effectiveness of the audit program.
The concept of partial reliance or acceptability applies only to the review and evaluation of the internal audit function. There may be situations in which the examiner detects certain weaknesses in the overall audit function or in the auditing procedures applicable to a specific area that are not of such a magnitude as to lead to a conclusion that the audit function is unacceptable In those situations, the examiner should conclude that the audit function is partially acceptable. Also, the examiner may conclude that the audit function is acceptable for a specific area, except that a specific audit procedure, such as reconcilement of bond issues, was not performed.
Although the OCC recognizes that the frequency of internal audit procedures should be based on an evaluation of risk associated with each area of audit interest, reliance on the work of internal auditors requires the OCC to establish general frequency guidelines applicable to most situations. Accordingly, the OCC expects that a suitable audit, including mandatory internal audit procedures, will be performed each calendar year and within 15 months of the previous audit as prescribed by 12 CFR 9.9.
External Audits Directors may use qualified outside accountants to perform audits. The examiner is interested in the work performed during external audits for three principal reasons. First, there will be situations where no internal audit work is being performed or where such work is deemed to be of limited value (partially acceptable) or no value (not acceptable) to the examiner. Second, the work performed by external auditors may affect the amount of testing to be performed by the examiner.
Third, the reports rendered by the external auditor often provide the examiner with information pertinent to the fiduciary activity.
The major considerations in evaluating the work of external auditors are similar to those applicable to internal auditors: namely, the competence and independence of the auditors and the adequacy of the audit.
Certified Public Accountants The following paragraphs contain selected information on the standards for reviewing the competence and independence of Cpas as well as the auditing standards for their audits of financial statements and preparation of reports. This discussion focuses on the standards promulgated by the American Institute of Certified Public Accountants (AICPA). All Cpas are not members of the AICPA, but most follow the professional standards adopted either by their respective CPA societies or the state agency issuing their licenses.
Independence of Certified Public Accountants Cpas must be independent of those for whom they work. Traditionally, independence has been defined as the ability to act with integrity and objectivity. The Concepts of Professional Ethics, which is a part of the Code of Professional Ethics of the AICPA, states, in part: When a CPA expresses an opinion on financial statements, not only the fact but also the appearance of integrity and objectivity is of particular importance.
For this reason, the profession has adopted rules to prohibit the expression of such an opinion when relationships exist which might pose such a threat to integrity and objectivity as to exceed the strength of countervailing forces and restraints. These relationinternal and External Audits Fiduciary Activities ships fall into two general categories: (a) certain financial relationships with clients, and (b) relationships in which the CPA is virtually part of management or an employee under management's control.
In accordance with Rule 101 on independence included in the Code of Professional Ethics and related AICPA interpretations, the independence of a CPA will be considered to be impaired, if, for example, during the period of his or her professional engagement, the CPA or his or her firm had: 1. Any direct or material indirect financial interest in the enterprise.
2. Any joint closely held business investment with the enterprise or any officer, director, or principal stockholder thereof which was material in relationship to the CPA or his or her firm's net worth.
3. A loan to or from the enterprise or any officer, director, or principal stockholder thereof. This proscription shall not apply to the following loans from a financial institution when made under normal lending procedures, terms and requirements: • Loans obtained by a CPA or his or her firm which are not material in relationship to the borrower's net worth.
• Other secured loans, except loans guaranteed by a CPA's firm which are otherwise unsecured.
Rule 101 is one of the Rules of Conduct of the AICPA Code of Professional Ethics. The Rules of Conduct are enforceable ethical standards of the AICPA. Additionally, the OCC and the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) require that all CPA firms that practice before them be independent. 12 CFR 11.7 and SEC Rule 2-01 of Regulation S-X state, in part: The OCC (Commission) will not recognize any certified public accountant as independent who is not in fact independent...
In determining whether an accountant may in fact be not independent, with respect to a particular person.
The OCC (Commission) will give appropriate consideration to all relevant circumstances, including evidence bearing on all relationships between the accountant and that person or any affiliate thereof, and will not confine itself to the relationship existing in connection with the filing of reports with the OCC (Commission).
Competence of Certified Public Accountants Certified public accountants are also required by their Code of Professional Ethics to perform examinations within the framework of generally accepted auditing standards. Auditing standards, as distinct from auditing procedures, pertain to the auditor's professional qualifications and his or her professional judgment.
Although the reports issued by the Cpas in their audit of fiduciary activities vary, for purposes of this discussion they have been limited to two general types. The first, commonly referred to as the short-form report, consists primarily of four paragraphs. The first paragraph identifies the scope and purpose of the audit, the second and third paragraphs describe the objectives and limitations of internal accounting controls, and the fourth paragraph contains the CPA's conclusion.
The second report is the long-form report. This report includes a more detailed description of the scope of the CPA's audit than that found in the short-form report.
Suggestions for improving the bank's internal controls, accounting procedures, and other matters may be included in either report, or be the subject of a separate communication in letter form.
Engagement Letters A bank normally requires that external auditors submit engagement letters to it or its directorate prior to beginning their work. Engagement letters include, among other things, the scope of the audit, the time period for the audit, and the reports expected to be rendered. In many cases, the highlights of those matters will be summarized in the body of the letter with greater detail being provided in schedules or appendixes to the letter.
Procedures may be specified by audit area. That is, the auditor may provide a capsulized description of procedures for inspection of account assets, review of account administration, etc.
Review of External Audits The OCC has concluded that only in unusual circumstances will it be necessary for the examiner to review the competence and independence of the CPA and the reliability of the CPA's audit work. However, there may be occasions when the examiner may have sufficient reason to question the independence of a CPA or the quality of his or her work. For example, the examiner may become aware that, during the period of a CPA's professional engagement, which includes the period covered by the financial statements on which the CPA has expressed an opinion, the CPA or a member of his or her firm: • Had a direct financial interest in the bank.
• Was connected with the bank in a capacity equivalent to that of a member of management or was a director of the bank.
• Maintained, completely or in part, the books and records of the bank and did not perform audit tests on such books and records.
• Had an unsecured loan from the bank that was considered to be material relative to the net worth of the borrower.
The list is by no means inclusive; however, in instances such as those the CPA would not have conformed to professional standards. Therefore, the examiner should Internal and External Audits Fiduciary Activities not rely on the CPA's work without a review of the procedures used in performing the audit. A review may require the examiner to request, through the bank, that the CPA make available appropriate working papers.
The CPA's working papers should be reviewed in light of the criteria discussed under "Internal Audit" and the procedures outlined in the Audit Function Questionnaire. If the procedures satisfy the examiner's requirements, he or she may rely on the work performed. The examiner may conclude, however, that the circumstances and their attendant risks preclude the acceptance of the CPA's work. Under such circumstances, the examiner should not rely on the audit. Since the OCC believes situations of this nature are rare, the examiner should contact the field office prior to requesting the CPA's working papers or concluding that the CPA's audit is not acceptable.
To determine the acceptability of a CPA audit, the examiner must assess the scope of the external audit by reviewing the last report issued by the CPA and any engagement letter provided to the bank. The bank and the examiner will be able to rely on the audit only if a long form audit report is provided. A short form audit report alone is not acceptable evidence that an audit meets OCC requirements. When a short form audit report is provided, the examiner should contact the external auditor to determine the audit scope. The trust audit committee should require the CPA to render a long-form report for future audits of fiduciary activity.
There will be situations in which the external audit is performed by individuals who are not certified public accountants. Such audits may be of a quality comparable to and be as reliable as audits performed by Cpas. The principal differences between Cpas and other external auditors relate to the absence of professional standards regarding competence and independence. Therefore, the examiner should apply the criteria discussed under "Internal Audits" in reviewing and evaluating the work of external auditors other than Cpas. Examples of other external auditors include auditors of the bank's holding company, auditors of correspondent banks, public accountants, and bank consultants.
Fiduciary Activities Audit The three separate audit functions required by 12 CFR 9.9 are: audit committee supervision, audit, and audit report. The requirements of the regulation will be met if the audit committee functions in an acceptable manner and appropriate audit procedures are performed in connection with an annual or continuous audit of the same scope. The audit should begin each calendar year and within 15 months of the initiation of the previous audit. If a continuous audit is performed, the date of the audit committee report described under heading C will be considered the audit date to determine conformance with this time limitation. An exception to the time limitation may be made only for the verification of assets.
The directors' audit of fiduciary activities must include: A. Audit Committee Supervision. 12 CFR 9.9 charges the bank's directors with the responsibility for auditing the fiduciary activities of the bank and directs that the responsibility be discharged through a committee appointed by the board. The audit committee must be composed of directors, exclusive of any active bank officers.
The fiduciary audit committee, among its other responsibilities must determine that the bank's fiduciary activities comply with policies established by the board of directors or its designated trust committee. Since the policy function should be separate from that of audit, directors should not serve on both committees simultaneously. However, in rare instances, directorates may be composed entirely of active bank officers. In such cases, the audit committee may consist of directors, who are active officers, other than officers responsible for fiduciary activities.
The committee cannot delegate its audit responsibilities, but may utilize any person or group of persons, including auditors of the bank, public accountants, attorneys or bank officers or employees to assist it in executing its audit functions. In no case, however, should persons assisting the audit committee participate in the review of their own work or that of a function over which they exercise supervisory control. All persons performing audit functions should be responsible only to the board of directors or the trust audit committee.
The audit committee shall ascertain, on the basis of observation and audit, whether the fiduciary activities of the bank are being administered in accordance with law, regulation and sound fiduciary principles. The committee should evaluate the policies, practices and controls employed by the bank to effect compliance and enforce correction of any violations, deficiencies, or weaknesses. In discharging its duties, the audit committee may rely on the evaluations and conclusions of internal and/or external auditors utilized by the committee in the performance or review of audit functions. It must ensure that appropriate parties have received the last OCC report of examination of fiduciary activities and any letters to or from the OCC to verify correction of exceptions, weaknesses, or deficiencies.
The committee should also confirm the correction of all exceptions, weaknesses, or deficiencies which may be brought to the bank's attention by internal and external auditors.
B. Audit. Outlined in the Audit Function Questionnaire are the OCC standards for audits, whether performed by internal or external auditors. Those standards are designed to promote audits which are comprehensive in scope and uniform in application. They are not intended, however, to limit the scope of fiduciary activity audits. Audits must include all procedures set forth in the Audit Function Questionnaire, if applicable. The Internal and External Audits Fiduciary Activities OCC allows flexibility to adjust the format of the audit program, or to limit testing in areas of minimal risk. The auditor must be able to demonstrate that the bank's exposure is sufficiently limited to justify minimal audit review.
The procedures outlined in the Audit Function Questionnaire are mandatory for all national banks. The auditor must base the sample size for each procedure on an analysis of risk. A test of management controls may be sufficient testing for a particular audit procedure. The auditor must perform all of these procedures each calendar year and within 15 months of the last audit.
C. Report. The audit committee must report formally in writing to the board of directors on the performance of its supervisory and audit functions. The report must set forth the committee's evaluations, conclusions and recommendations on the condition of the bank's fiduciary activities, and the effectiveness of its policies, practices and controls. It also must include a specific statement of the committee's conclusion as to whether fiduciary activities are being administered in accordance with law, 12 CFR 9 and sound fiduciary principles. The report must be accompanied by audit reports prepared by internal and/or external auditors.
The audit committee must ensure that auditor's reports are sufficiently descriptive and reflect the scope of the audit.
If continuous audits are employed, the audit committee must make a formal written report to the board of directors each calendar year and within 15 months of the previous report. Periodic reports to the committee by auditors who perform continuous audit functions must be consolidated with the committee's report. This annual reporting requirement is not intended to preclude more frequent reporting to the board when the committee considers such action appropriate. Rather, it is designed to establish a date of audit in conformity with the calendar year and 15-month time limitation imposed by 12 CFR 9.9.

Asset Administration Fiduciary Activities The primary function of a fiduciary is the management of property for others. The potential investments for consideration by fiduciary institutions have expanded considerably in recent years. Asset administration and its component work programs focus on such complex issues as the selection, purchase, management and sale of assets to meet the needs of various classes of beneficiaries. This section addresses the administration of the different forms of assets that may be found in fiduciary accounts.
Fiduciary asset administration is regulated largely by prudent person standards, common law principles, state and federal laws and regulations, and industry practices. However, a trustee is provided with considerable latitude to express an individualized investment style and develop investment alternatives.
One role of the trustee in asset administration is to identify assets which may be appropriate investment vehicles. The trustee must also administer those fiduciary assets for the exclusive benefit of the beneficiaries.
In a true fiduciary relationship, the trustee has legal ownership of the trust property and is accountable to the beneficial owners for management of those assets.
The trustee must be concerned with both the legality and prudence of each investment. Under the common law of most states, a trustee must use the same amount of care, skill and caution in making investments as a prudent person would to preserve the estate and the amount and regularity of income. However, state law may impose stricter standards on a fiduciary, and under common law principles, a corporate fiduciary may generally be held to higher standards of care, skill and caution, than one trustee.
The ultimate responsibility for the proper exercise of fiduciary powers lies with the bank's board of directors.
The board may assign to officers or committees the authority to make and monitor investment decisions.
The extent of such assignment will vary with the size, volume of activity, and complexity of individual trust departments and may include establishment of policies, approval of discretionary actions, and periodic review of accounts. Accurate records should be maintained of investment decisions and review procedures.
The bank should have an investment policy approved by the board of directors, which includes an overall statement of investment philosophy and details the responsibilities of committees and officers involved in the investment process. It should address, among other things, investment quality standards and procedures to evaluate asset quality, portfolio guidelines, diversification requirements, risk assessment procedures, account reviews, use of common trust funds, broker/ dealer selection, and safeguards against conflicts of interest and self-dealing.

Investments-Marketable Securities Fiduciary Activities The term "marketable securities" refers generally to securities in active trading markets where they may be converted readily into cash. They include common stocks, preferred stocks, government securities, corporate bonds, municipal securities and money market instruments. The relative liquidity of those investments generally allows the trustee to convert them into cash more readily than other types of investments.
In general, over extended periods of time, common stock offers potential for both the greatest rewards and the greatest risks among the types of investment securities. Common stock is an ownership security. It guarantees the shareholder the right to share in the earnings of the company through future dividends and price appreciation.
Several advantages accrue to the institutional investor by using common stocks to achieve investment objectives. Needs of income beneficiaries may be met by income from dividend payments. Capital appreciation of stocks increases the value of the trust for the remainderman. Although the current dividend yield may be low, potential for capital gain may be great. Common stocks that are registered on an organized securities exchange also are traded in a more efficient market as opposed to some other forms of investment. Options may be written on some issues for additional income. By selling call options against a stock, the investor receives the premium income, which is added to the yield of the stock. The effect of writing the option is to increase the investor's yield without substantially increasing the risk of investment.
There are four attributes of preferred stock which make that form of ownership attractive to investors: • The preference of the investor's dividends on the earnings of the company.
• The preference to a claim on assets over common stock in the unusual cases of liquidation, reorganization, refinancing, or adjustment of a company's capital account.
• The partial (85 percent) tax exemption of preferred dividends if owned by another corporation.
• Preferred stock may be convertible into common stock.
Some investors consider preferred stock a hybrid security, possessing all the disadvantages of debt securities and none of the advantages of common stock. However, high-grade preferred stocks have stability of income and security of principal. They do not share in the business or market risks to the extent of common stock.
They do share in interest rate and inflation risks.
Debentures convertible to common stock are another investment alternative. Debentures are unsecured bonds protected only by the general credit of the borrowing corporation. The flexibility offered by a convertible debenture may be advantageous to a particular investment program. Normally, those securities combine the characteristics of debentures and common stocks.
There also may be a yield sacrifice in comparison to nonconvertible debentures as a result of the conversion feature. Convertibles can limit interest rate risk, i.e., a rise in interest rates depresses the price of fixed income securities, and provides an opportunity to participate in potential capital stock appreciation through stock conversion privileges. If the conversion privilege is not exercised, the bond will pay par at maturity.
The convertible features of both preferred stock and debentures may be complex. If the trustee holds convertible securities, adequate provisions for monitoring call provisions and changes in conversion ratios should be in place. Investment services may be used to monitor those factors.
In making investment decisions regarding common and preferred stocks, and preferred stocks and debentures convertible into common stocks, the following factors should be considered: • Quality and depth of management of the company.
• Financial condition and position of the company in its industry.
• Quality of earnings and historical growth of the company.
• Assessment of future prospects of the company in terms of sales and earnings, projected price/ earnings and price/dividend ratios, existing and potential competition, etc., based on comparisons with peer companies in the same industry.
• Anticipated availability of raw materials to sustain future growth.
• Anticipated legislation, potential governmental intervention, and labor unrest.
• Trend of per capita use of the company's products.
• Demonstrated ability of the company to develop new products and/or upgrade existing ones.
• Ability of the market to absorb sales of securities, i.e., whether the security is traded in a "thin" market, as is the case with many insurance and bank stocks, and municipal bonds.
Litigation pending against the company.
Most of those considerations are more easily researched for "seasoned" corporations than for new issues offered to the public.
