Portfolio Management Fiduciary Activities Portfolio management involves establishing and maintaining a portfolio that maximizes returns relative to an investor's ability to bear investment risk. It is an ongoing process by which an investor's objectives, constraints, and preferences are identified to develop explicit investment objectives. Market conditions, relative asset values, and the investor's circumstances are then monitored. Portfolio adjustments are made, as appropriate, in response to significant changes in any and all of the relevant variables.
The goal of the portfolio manager is to manage risk in pursuit of the determined investment objectives of individual accounts. Each account's circumstances and needs should be defined in terms of acceptable levels of risk and rate of return. The portfolio manager must try to meet the needs of the beneficiaries, considering the terms of the governing instrument, restraints of local and federal law, and applicable rules and regulations.
In developing an investment program, the fiduciary must consider specific investment constraints as well as the overall investment objectives of an account. The following factors must be considered in determining overall portfolio strategies.
Investment Authority The fiduciary must manage investments in accordance with the terms of the governing instrument, which is the primary determinant of a fiduciary's powers and duties.
The fiduciary may have sole authority in making investment decisions or joint authority with another co-fiduciary. Where required by the governing instrument, an authorized party may have to approve an investment decision or the trustee may have to consult with other designated persons. If the instrument is silent as to authorized investments, the trustee is guided by provisions of local law. The terms of the instrument may set forth various types of unusual or restrictive investment provisions, such as: • Prohibit the purchase or sale of a particular security or one in a particular industry.
• Require the fiduciary to purchase only certain authorized securities or limit investments in one asset or asset category to a specified amount to avoid a concentration.
Whenever the corporate fiduciary's actions are contrary to provisions of the governing instrument, local law or applicable rules and regulations, it is exposed to potential liability through beneficiary litigation.
Exercise of Reasonable Care The fiduciary must be able to demonstrate that it acted properly under the standard for fiduciary investments established by local law in its jurisdiction. Although standards for fiduciary investment vary from state to state (some state statutes specify "legal lists" of authorized fiduciary investments), the overwhelming majority of states have adopted some variation of the common law rule of prudence. The prudent person rule was originally stated in the Harvard College v. Amory decision and has been restated by various authoritative sources. Although language of individual state statutes vary, they follow the general theme of the prudency test as it has evolved under the common law.
Harvard College v. Amory stated the prudent man rule as follows: All that can be required of a trustee to invest is that he shall conduct himself faithfully and exercise sound discretion. He is to observe how men of prudence, discretion and intelligence manage their own affairs, not in regard to speculation, but in regard to the permanent disposition of their funds considering the probable income as well as the probable safety of the capital to be invested. [Harvard College v. Amory, 9 Pick (26 Mass.), 446 (1830).] The rule was stated later by Professor Austin Scott as follows: The only general rule which can be laid down as to investments is that the trustee is under a duty to make such investment as a prudent man would make of his own property having primarily in view the preservation of the estate and the amount and regularity of the income to be derived. In various forms this rule has been stated in innumerable cases. It involved three elements, namely care, skill and caution. The trustee must exercise a reasonable degree of care in selecting investments. He must exercise a reasonable degree of skill in making the selection. He must, in addition, exercise the caution which a prudent man would exercise where the primary consideration is the preservation of the funds invested. (A. Scott, Law of Trusts, 3d ed., 1967) Employee benefit accounts are governed by the Employee Retirement Income Security Act of 1974 (ERISA).
ERISA establishes a prudent person rule for employee benefit accounts, known as the federal prudent person rule, which differs from the common law standard. The major distinguishing difference is that the prudence concept is applied to the total portfolio rather than to individual investments within the portfolio.
Beneficiary Needs In structuring account portfolios, the trustee should consider carefully the personal circumstances of the beneficiaries. Once the needs of the beneficiaries are established, the trustee can establish specific portfolio objectives. The more important considerations in determining their needs are: • Need for Income. A fiduciary must determine the amount of income that the beneficiaries need from the portfolio to maintain their desired standard of living. Often, this determination involves weighing the needs of income beneficiaries against the legitiportfolio Management Fiduciary Activities mate interest of the remainder beneficiaries in preserving and increasing the account assets.
Strategy must then be formulated to produce a minimum level of income, even under adverse conditions. The fiduciary must be aware of beneficiaries' income and expenses from other sources and maintain ongoing communication with the beneficiaries.
• Need for Principal Distributions. The need or likelihood that principal distributions will be made in the future also must be considered by the portfolio manager.
• Tax Status of Beneficiaries. If the beneficiaries or the fiduciary account is in a high tax bracket, investment in tax-exempt securities, such as municipal bonds or other forms of tax-shelters, should be considered. Although tax consequences of retaining, purchasing and selling securities should not be the primary determinant for investment decisions, the ability to minimize taxes may have a significant effect on an account's total income.
• Loss of Purchasing Power. Due to inflation, the loss of purchasing power in recent years has varied from 3 percent to over 10 percent annually. Unless a fiduciary account is established for a short period of time, the fiduciary must consider investments which will appreciate and preserve the purchasing power of fiduciary accounts.
• Human Dimension of Portfolio Management. A fiduciary must keep in mind the temperament of individual beneficiaries. Investment policy may be influenced by what individual beneficiaries want, fear and hope for. Investment objectives that the beneficiaries indicate they want to follow, may be quite different from those they are able to live with. The knowledge that the beneficiary will be disturbed by a loss in market value may be one reason for the fiduciary to minimize that risk. The extent to which investment policies should be modified by considerations relating to the temperament of the beneficiaries is a question of judgment.
• Estimated Termination Date of Account. The investment objectives established for fiduciary accounts must relate to the estimated length of time the fiduciary account will be in existence. Establishing long-term investment goals for fiduciary accounts which will terminate within a relatively short period would be inappropriate.
Risk Management Over the past 30 years, the focus of fiduciary investment management has gradually shifted from a primary emphasis on individual asset selection to a more balanced emphasis on portfolio diversification and the interrelationship of individual securities, and their particular characteristics within the portfolio as a whole.
Thus, the theory of structuring a portfolio has significantly altered the practice of investment management.
A successful portfolio manager must structure investment portfolios which meet the investment objectives of the account while providing adequate protection against risk.
There are many different types of risks; however, for purposes of this discussion, they can be classified into three general areas: market, industry and issue.
Market risks are inherent and cannot be eliminated completely. However, they can be managed through appropriate portfolio diversification, such as multiple market portfolios. Cyclical movements in a particular market sector can be offset with investments in other markets experiencing dissimilar market fluctuations.
This is an important reason behind diversifying internationally. The stock markets and currencies of different countries fluctuate, but at different times and for different reasons.
In a dynamic economy, certain industries may experience superior growth in both sales and profits while others sustain relative decline. Products of particular industries can become obsolete in the face of rapid technological development. Decisions to invest or retain investments in particular industries should be evaluated periodically in light of current industry developments and long term industry trends.
Despite the relative prosperity of a particular industry, individual companies within that industry may fail to meet anticipated investment performance. Earnings of individual companies may suffer because of internal corporate problems, such as a decline in management capabilities. In an attempt to limit the risk of a particular issue, investors may chose to concentrate on industry leaders to reduce the likelihood of serious earnings decline.
A fiduciary account should be managed continuously to achieve its established investment objectives. When individual accounts are reviewed, consideration must be given to whether securities should be retained, sold or purchased. Policies regarding portfolio composition also are influenced by the level of prices prevailing for different types of investments and the current structure of security yields. Changes in prices and yields often produce the need to change the asset mix. In a majority of portfolios, asset mix is one of the more important determinants of performance. Although many investment managers concentrate on stock selection, specific stocks held may not have nearly as much impact on total performance as will the overall level of equity commitment.
There are various investment strategies which may be used by a fiduciary. A conservative strategy is designed to minimize risk. It is intended to provide stability of income and principal, and afford as much opportunity for appreciation in principal as may be consistent with portfolio objectives. Aggressive strategies are designed primarily to provide large financial returns to the individportfolio Management Fiduciary Activities ual portfolio, either in terms of yield or in appreciation of principal. A greater degree of investment risk and wider tolerance for losses is required with that approach. Additional investment considerations which may affect the trustees selection of appropriate investment policies include: • Safety of principal.
Capital appreciation.
Marketability.
Letters of administration issued by the probate court are required for the bank to commence administration of the estate. The executor acquires his/her powers, duties, and responsibilities by appointment of that court.
Administration of the estate continues until a final accounting has been filed and approved, and the executor is discharged formally.
When appointed by the court, the executor assumes responsibility for safekeeping all assets of the estate.
Therefore, the fiduciary must immediately identify and assume control of all assets to assure that they are adequately safeguarded and insured. In addition, the executor is required to file an inventory and appraisal with the appropriate court. The inventory and appraisal aid in establishing the value of estate assets for tax and distribution purposes.
Once the inventory and appraisal have been filed, the executor must pay all taxes, debts and administrative expenses and distribute the net estate in accordance with the terms of the will and/or local law. The executor has the power to sell assets to pay claims, taxes and administrative expenses. Depending upon local law and the terms of the will, the executor may invest assets during administration. The executor should pay all legitimate claims and expenses, as soon as they are presented for payment. Occasionally, an estate may be insolvent; that is, the claims may exceed the value of the estate. In those instances, the state's probate law will specify the priority for paying claims, administrative expenses, and support to the family during administration. After all expenses chargeable to the estate have been paid, including appropriate federal and state taxes, the executor can begin to close the estate.
The termination of the estate requires a final accounting to the court. After the court has approved the final accounting, it will issue an order, often called the Decree of Distribution, directing the executor to distribute the net estate to the beneficiaries. The executor is responsible for delivering the assets to the beneficiaries and for properly transferring ownership. When the distribution is complete, the executor will present receipts and a supplemental final accounting to the court requesting that the estate be closed and that the executor be discharged. When the probate court finds that the supplemental final accounting and receipts from all beneficiaries are in order, it will issue an Order of Discharge.
A guardian is appointed by the court to protect the estate of an person who is mentally or physically incapacitated or who is not of legal age. A guardian may be responsible for the care of the person and their property or the court may appoint a separate guardian for both the ward's person and his/her property. A national bank trustee should normally only be appointed guardian of the property.
Under a guardianship arrangement, the ward is the beneficial owner of the property, but the guardian has certain powers derived from the court for those assets.
The guardian's two main objectives in managing the protected person's property are to meet the needs of the person and conserve the assets. As the guardianship is subject to court supervision, accountings must be filed in accordance with local law.
The guardianship terminates on the death of the ward, a minor reaching legal age, or when a ward is declared competenm by a court.
Personal Trusts A trust is a fiduciary relationship by which legal title to property is held by one person or corporation for the benefit of another. A trust separates the responsibility of ownership of specific property from the benefit of ownership. The person who has the responsibility of ownership is called the trustee and holds legal title to the specific property; the person who has the benefit of ownership is called the beneficiary and holds equitable title. The specific property which is the subject of this divided ownership is called the trust res or the trust property.
The two most common types of trusts administered by a bank are living (inter vivos) and testamentary trusts.
The primary distinction between the two is the manner Account Administration-Personal Fiduciary Activities in which they are created. Living trusts are created by an agreement between a living person and a trustee. A testamentary trust is created by the provisions of a decedent's will after appropriate court approval is received. A probate court usually has continuing jurisdiction over a testamentary trust.
Responsibilities of a trustee include: • Preservation of the assets composing the trust.
• Distribution of income to designated beneficiaries.
Counseling the beneficiaries.
The fiduciary's powers should be stated clearly in the trust instrument. The absence of clearly defined powers may cause uncertainty and delay, impair investment performance, and prevent the trustee from taking actions in the best interests of the beneficiaries. Local law may give the trustee certain powers to administer the trust in the absence of clearly defined instructions in the governing instrument. Common provisions within the governing instrument include the power to purchase, retain and sell fiduciary assets. Specific powers should be included for the management of real estate and other miscellaneous assets.
The instrument should provide for: • The use of nominee names.
An inter vivos trust may be revocable or irrevocable depending upon the terms of the governing instrument.
A revocable trust is one in which the settlor reserves the right to terminate the trust at any time and have the trust property transferred to his/her possession. An irrevocable trust is one in which the settlor does not reserve the right to reclaim the property. Under common law, if the instrument is silent regarding revocation, the trust is assumed to be irrevocable.
A testamentary trust is, by its nature, irrevocable, as it is created by terms of a will upon the death of an person.
However, provisions of a trust agreement may designate persons with the power to terminate the trust or to appoint alternate trustees.
Termination of a trust is governed by specific provisions of the governing instrument. Several states have adopted statutes which limit the duration of trusts. In addition to state statutes, the common law rule against perpetuities should be considered when establishing a trust. The rule against perpetuities relates to the time period within which a future interest must vest.
Agencies Although an agency is a fiduciary relationship that may involve the management of property for others, there are two basic distinctions between it and a trust relationship: • The agent ordinarily is not the owner of the property for the benefit of the principal and as such, does not hold title to the property. A trustee holds title to the property for the beneficiaries.
An agency usually is revocable at the option of the principal and is revoked by the death of either party.
A trust may be irrevocable and continue beyond the death of a settlor or beneficiary.
There are several different types of agency accounts: • Managing agency-Generally authorizes the bank to exercise full investment discretion.
- Directed agency The agent acts in an advisory capacity only and executes investment changes only upon the direction of the principal.
• Safekeeping and custody - The agent maintains custody of assets, does not act in an advisory capacity and generally does not execute investment changes for the account.
Generally, duties of the agent may include: • Collecting the principal of matured or called bonds.
• Accepting possession of securities for safekeeping.
• Notifying customers of calls, subscription rights, defaults, etc.

Account Administration-Employee Benefit Accounts Fiduciary Activities Employee benefit funds have experienced extraordinary growth in the past decade. Assets of these funds.
dominate the institutional investor market and present a rapidly expanding market for fiduciary services.
The major law applicable to employee benefit plans and trusts is the Employee Benefit Income Security Act of 1974 (ERISA). This law was enacted to protect the rights of employees by imposing sweeping reforms in the creation, operation and administration of employee benefit plans. ERISA establishes specific requirements for plan reporting, disclosure, participation, vesting, funding and fiduciary responsibility. One of the major objectives in the examination of employee benefit accounts is to ensure that the bank is administering plans in accordance with the fiduciary standards of ERISA. Banks that administer plans or accounts subject to ERISA are liable for violations of the act and for the mismanagement of such plans and accounts. The Department of Labor is the primary agency given responsibility for enforcing provisions of ERISA.
ERISA, section 504, grants the U.S. Department of Labor general authority to investigate fiduciaries and service providers to fiduciary accounts. This investigative authority would be limited to information which might assist DOL in determining whether Title I of ERISA has been violated. Banks are reminded that OCC reports of examination remain the property of the Comptroller of the Currency and may not be disclosed except as specifically authorized by the Comptroller. Banks are further reminded that customer financial records may be subject to the Right to Financial Privacy Act of 1978.
Banks should consult their counsel if they have questions on the Right to Financial Privacy Act's procedural requirements.
The Department of Labor has been granted subpoena authority by ERISA. Banks may wish to request the Pension and Welfare Benefits Administration (PWBA) to issue a subpoena for bank records and documents concerning employee plans. A subpoena will provide the bank with evidence, if requested by plan sponsors, of PWBA's right to examine plan records.
Roles of the Bank Trustee Under ERISA, a corporate trustee is a fiduciary with respect to a plan. Upon appointment as trustee, the bank is generally responsible for the exclusive management and control of plan assets. However, the plan may provide that the trustee is subject to the direction of a named fiduciary or management of plan assets may be delegated to one or more investment managers.
Profit Sharing Plans A profit sharing plan is established by an employer to provide for employee participation in the profits of the corporation. Contributions to a profit sharing plan are based on a pre-defined formula such as a percentage of profits, or at the discretion of the board of directors of the employer. However, the plan must provide for allocating among the participants contributions made to the plan.
Money Purchase Plan Employer contributions are determined based upon a percentage of compensation for specific persons.
As with the profit sharing plan, contributions are allocated to the participants and the benefits for each participant are derived from the amounts contributed to each account.
Stock Bonus Plans Stock bonus plans are established by an employer to provide benefits similar to those of a profit sharing plan, except that contributions by the employer are not necessarily dependent upon profits, and the benefits are distributed in the stock of the employer company.
These plans are designed to invest primarily in qualified employer securities.
Types of stock bonus plans include Employee Stock Ownership Plans (Esops) and Payroll Stock Ownership Plans (Paysops). Those plans will be discussed later in this section.
Self Employed Retirement Plans Self employed retirement plans are called HR-10 or Keogh plans. A self-employed person may establish a qualified pension or profit sharing plan. Those plans are generally subject to the same benefit and contribution limits as qualified corporate plans as a result of the Tax Equity and Fiscal Responsibility Act of 1982.
Individual Retirement Plan Account (Iras) Employees and self-employed persons receiving compensation can establish their own individual retirement plan, even if they may be covered already under qualified plans. Persons can contribute up to $2,000 per year in a qualified individual retirement plan or $2,250 in a spousal IRA. However, the Tax Reform Act of 1986 imposed certain restrictions on the deduction of IRA contributions. Employers also may provide retirement benefits to employees by making contributions to an employee's IRA instead of maintaining a more complicated qualified plan. Those plans are referred to as Simplified Employee Pension Plans or SEP's.
401(K) Plans-Deferred Profit Sharing Participants of qualified profit sharing or stock bonus plans with cash or deferred features can elect to receive direct cash payments from their employers or defer employer contributions on their behalf to a trust. This arrangement, referred to as a 401(K) Plan, may take the form of a salary reduction plan providing for employer contributions only if the participant elects to reduce compensation or forego a pay increase.

Account Administration-Employee Benefit Accounts Fiduciary Activities Fiduciary Responsibilities Under ERISA, a fiduciary generally is defined to include any person or organization who has any power of control, management, or disposition over the funds or other property or any employee benefit fund. ERISA establishes specific rules governing the conduct of all plan fiduciaries and specifies certain transactions which fiduciaries are prohibited from engaging in. The major provisions in Part 4 of Title 1 of ERISA, which establishes fiduciary responsibilities, are outlined as follows.
Section 401- Coverage Not every employee benefit plan is subject to ERISA's fiduciary responsibility rules. Specifically exempted from the fiduciary requirements of ERISA are governmental plans, certain church plans, plans established solely to comply with Workmen's Compensation, unemployment compensation or disability insurance laws and unfunded nonpension benefit plans.
Section 402-Fiduciary Responsibility Every employee benefit plan covered by ERISA's fiduciary responsibility provisions must be established and maintained in accordance with a written plan.
Each plan must: • Specify a procedure for establishing and carrying out the funding policy of the plan.
• Formally delegate to named fiduciaries responsibilities for the operation and administration of the plan.
• Specify the basis of contributions to and payments from the plan.
The employee benefit plan may also contain certain optional provisions including: Permission for a person or organization to serve in multiple fiduciary capacities.
• Authorization for a named fiduciary responsible for the management of plan assets to hire an investment manager or adviser.
A special rule is applicable to individual account plans where the participant exercises independent control over the assets in his or her individual account. In that case, the individual is not to be regarded as a fiduciary and other fiduciaries are not liable for any losses as a result of the proper exercise of control by the participant. For example, if the participant instructs the trustee to invest the full balance of his or her account in a single stock, the trustee would not be held liable for any loss due to failure to diversify. However, the investments of the individual plan must not contradict the terms of the plan, and if the plan on its face prohibits such investments, the trustee could not follow the instructions without incurring liability.
Section 405- Co-Fiduciary Liability Under certain circumstances, plan fiduciaries may be liable for a breach of fiduciary duty even if they play no direct role in the activity constituting the breach. Fiduciaries are held liable if they participate knowingly in or knowingly undertake to conceal acts or omissions by other fiduciaries that they know constitute a breach of ERISA's fiduciary responsibility rules.
Section 406 - Prohibited Transactions ERISA attempts to eliminate fiduciary misconduct and self-dealing by prohibiting certain specific transactions between plans and related parties. Prohibited transactions under ERISA involve the direct or indirect: • Sale, exchange, or leasing of any property between the plan and a party in interest.
Lending of money or other extension of credit between the plan and a party in interest.
Furnishing of goods, services, or other facilities between the plan and a party in interest.
• Transfer to or use of plan assets by or for the benefit of a party in interest.
• Receipt of any consideration for the personal account of a party in interest (self-dealing).
In addition, ERISA specifically prohibits a fiduciary from holding employer stock or real property in violation of Section 407(a), or from engaging in any other transaction with an person or entity whose interests are adverse to the plan participants or beneficiaries. There are stiff penalties for fiduciaries who permit party-in-interest transactions in violation of Section 406 of ERISA.
Section 407-10 Percent Limitation for Employer Securities and Real Property ERISA Section 407 generally limits to 10 percent of the total plan assets, the acquisition and holding by a plan of qualifying employer securities and qualifying real property. However an eligible individual account plan (profit sharing plans, stock bonus plans, thrift or savings plans or ESOPS) may invest a larger percentage of assets in such holdings. The individual account plan must provide specifically for the investment of more than 10 percent of its assets in such holdings and specify the percentage.
The term qualifying employer securities is defined as stock or marketable obligations of the employer. The term "qualifying employer real property" is defined as real property leased to the employer which is dispersed geographically and improvements thereon are suitable or adaptable without excessive cost for more than one use.

Account Administration-Employee Benefit Accounts Fiduciary Activities Section 408 tions - Exemptions from Prohibited Transacsection 408 of ERISA exempts from the prohibited transaction rules certain party-in-interest transactions.
Those exemptions include: • Contracting or making reasonable arrangements with a party-in-interest for office space, legal, accounting or other services necessary for the establishment or operation of a plan, if no more than reasonable compensation is paid.
• Loans to an employee stock ownership trust provided: -The loan is primarily for the benefit of the participants and beneficiaries of the plan.
-The loan is at an interest rate which is not in excess of a reasonable rate.
• Investment in own bank interest bearing deposits if deposits bear a reasonable rate of interest, and if the plan covers only employers of such bank or if such investment is expressly authorized by provisions of the plan.
• Use of own bank collective investment funds when expressly permitted by the terms of the plan or when directed by a fiduciary or the bank who has authority to manage or control the assets of the plan.
• Loans made by the plan to parties-in-interest who are participants or beneficiaries of the plan if such loans: — Are available to all participants on a fair and equitable basis.
Have a reasonable rate of return.
Are adequately secured.
-Are made in accordance with specific provisions of the plan.
In addition, provisions of the Tax Equity and Fiscal Responsibility Act of 1982 (TEFRA) place certain limits on the amounts which can be borrowed and maximum repayment terms. If the participants' vested interest is: • $10,000 or less, the entire vested interest is available.
• Between $10,000 and $20,000, $10,000 is available.
• Between $20,000 and $100,000, 50 percent of the vested interest is available.
The loan by its terms must be repaid within five years.
However, that time limit does not apply if the loan is to be used for a principal residence of the employee or of the employee's spouse and children, and not as a means to acquire investment property. TEFRA provisions apply to all loans made to participants after August 13, 1982.
The Tax Reform Act of 1986 significantly changed the treatment of participant loans governed by the Internal Revenue Code, Section 72(p). For loans made, extended or revised after December 31, 1986, the Tax Reform Act requires that plan loans be amortized in even payments, made at least quarterly, over the term of the loan. If the loan is not to be a taxable distribution, it must bear a reasonable rate of interest and mature in five years. The new law changed the outstanding balance on all loans to the participant which had been onehalf of the present value of the participant's nonforfeitable accrued benefit or $50,000, whichever was less.
The new limit reduced that amount by the highest outstanding balance owed by the participant during the prior 12 months.
The repayment period may be longer than five years only if the loan is used to acquire a principal residence of the participant. Prior law allowed an extended loan period for acquisition, construction or rehabilitation of a principal residence for the participant or a member of the participant's family.
Certain participants cannot claim tax deductions for the interest they pay on loans from qualified plans.
Interest paid by a key employee (as defined in Code Section 416) or any loan or interest paid by any employee if the loan is secured by elective deferral amounts in a cash-or-deferred plan or Section 403(b) plan will be treated as nondeductible employee contributions to the plan.
The examiner should review loans from employee benefit trust accounts to ensure that they comply with ERISA and the Internal Revenue Code.
The Secretary of Labor may also grant specific exemptions upon application of a fiduciary from all or part of the restrictions imposed by ERISA, Section 406 and 407(a), where administratively feasible, in the best interest of plan participants and beneficiaries, and protective of the rights of participants and beneficiaries of such plans.
Prohibited Transaction Exemption 81-6 Lending employee benefit plan securities to parties in interest or to disqualified persons would normally be a prohibited transaction. (Section 406(a) of ERISA and section 4975 of the Internal Revenue Code of 1986.) However, if certain conditions specified in the exemption are met, PTE 81-6 permits an employee benefit plan to lend plan securities to broker-dealers registered under the Securities Exchange Act of 1934 (1934 act) or to banks, even though they may be considered parties in interest or disqualified persons. Those conditions relate to: (1) the transfer and market value of the collateral; (2) financial information on the borrower; (3) Account Administration-Employee Benefit Accounts Fiduciary Activities written loan agreements; (4) fees; (5) whether the borrower or any affiliates exercises control over plan assets or provides investment advice to the plan; and (6) loan termination provisions.
This exemption was effective January 23, 1981. It was amended in May 13, 1987, to add broker dealers in government securities, who are exempt from registration under 15(a)(1) of the 1934 act, to the list of securities borrowers to whom employee benefit plans may lend securities.
However, even if a transaction is exempted under PTE 81-6, the plan fiduciary is not relieved from other provisions of ERISA and the Internal Revenue Code. Section 404 of ERISA requires that a fiduciary discharge his or her duties solely in the interests of the participants and beneficiaries of the plan, and section 401(a) of the Internal Revenue Code mandates that the plan be operated for the exclusive benefit of the employees and their beneficiaries.
Employee Stock Ownership Plans An employee stock ownership plan (ESOP) is an individual account plan that is either a qualified stock bonus plan or a combination qualified stock bonus and qualified money purchase plan. It must be designed to invest primarily in qualifying employer securities and meet other requirements specified by IRS regulations. Esops are defined contribution plans and must provide separate accounts for each participant, and base benefits solely on amounts contributed to each individual account. The principal feature that differentiates ESOPS from conventional stock bonus, profit sharing or thrift plans is their ability to generate corporate capital through tax-deductible loans.
As stock bonus plans, ESOPS offer employees a stake in the employer corporation through stock ownership.
ESOPS offer employers the ability to leverage or generate capital for use in business, through exempt loans.
Generally, any direct or indirect loan of money or extension of credit between a plan and a disqualified person is a prohibited transaction. A leveraged ESOP is exempt from this prohibition for loans made primarily for the benefit of plan participants and beneficiaries, at reasonable interest rates, and secured by collateral consisting only of qualifying employer securities. An ESOP must be so designated in the plan document. It may be established alone, or as part of an existing qualified pension, profit sharing, or stock bonus plan.
An existing plan may be converted to an ESOP as long as the conversion involves no compromise of fiduciary duties and no violation of the exclusive benefit rule.
Employer stock may be acquired by the plan either: • Through direct employer contributions of the stock itself.
By the plan purchasing the stock from the employer with cash contributions provided by the employer.
• With the proceeds of a loan to the plan that is secured by the employer's stock.
An ESOP that borrows to acquire employer stock is referred to as a "leveraged ESOP" and is an extremely effective corporate financing tool.
A plan will not qualify as an ESOP unless: • Participants have the right to demand benefits be distributed in the form of employer securities.
• There is a built-in put option requiring the employer to buy back, at a fair valuation, and within a specified time, any distributed employer securities that are not publicly traded or that are subject to a trading limitation.
• Certain voting rights attached to the employer securities are passed through to the participants.
The valuation of qualifying employer securities acquired by an ESOP with exempt-loan proceeds may be necessary for: • Rights of first refusal.
Valuations must be made in good faith, based on all relevant factors used to determine fair market value. In the case of dealings with a disqualified person, value is determined as of the transaction date. If no disqualified person is involved, value is determined as of the most recent valuation date under the plan. Appraisals performed independently, at least once a year, by a person who customarily appraises securities and who is independent of any party exercising a put option or right of first refusal, are good faith determinations of value for transactions not involving a disqualified person.
The Tax Reform Act of 1986 changed the ESOP rules on distributions, deductions, and exclusions. Generally, the favorable tax treatment that ESOPS enjoyed before tax reform has been retained and in some cases expanded.

Distribution Rules Under the new law, unless an employee elects otherwise, distributions from Esops must begin within one year after disability, death, or retirement, and within five years after separation from service. Unless the plan allows a participant to elect a longer period, it must provide distributions over a period not exceeding five years. However if the participant's balance is greater than $500,000, a one-year period may be permitted for each $100,000 or fraction thereof, of the participant's balance, up to a total distribution period of 10 years.

Account Administration-Employee Benefit Accounts Fiduciary Activities Put Option Requirements The new law retains the prior requirement that benefits be distributed as employer securities and that a participant receiving distributions of employer securities from a leveraged ESOP be given a put option for those not readily tradeable. (Section 409 of IRC of 1986.) The new law also extends the put option requirement for employer securities that are not readily tradeable to stock bonus plans. (Section 401(a)(3)) Investment Diversification Section 401(a)(28)(A) and (B) of the Internal Revenue Code was added by the Tax Reform Act of 1986 to require that employees under an ESOP who have attained age 55 and completed at least 10 years of service be given an opportunity to diversify the investment in their own accounts. To the extent that a participant elects that option, an ESOP must offer at least three investment alternatives that conform with regulations to be issued.
Independent Appraiser The valuation of employer securities that are not readily tradeable on an established securities market, for all activities covered by the ESOP, must be made by an independent appraiser. The term "independent appraiser" means any appraiser meeting requirements similar to those prescribed by the regulations under Section 170(a)(1). (Section 401(a)(28)(c)) The investment-based (TRASOP) employee stock ownership credit was terminated with respect to transfers of employer securities to plans, for taxable years beginning after December 31, 1982. In its place, the Economic Recovery Tax Act of 1981 implemented the payroll-based (PAYSOP) employee stock ownership credit. As a result, electing employers get an income tax credit for contributions based on a percentage of the compensation of employees covered by the plan.
A PAYSOP, like its predecessor TRASOP, is a defined contribution profit sharing, stock bonus or combination money purchase-stock bonus plan. Plan assets attributable to the tax credit must be 100 percent invested in employer securities. However, contributions in excess of the allowable credit need not be invested in employer securities, as long as a separate accounting is kept.
Under PAYSOPS, participants must be given the right to demand distributions in the form of employer securities.
A put option must obligate the plan to redeem distributed securities that are not readily saleable on the open market. Voting rights must be passed through to participants.
The Tax Reform Act repealed the major tax advantage for establishing a PAYSOP by moving up the expiration date of the payroll-based tax credit. The credit for companies establishing a PAYSOP equaled 1/2 of 1 percent of the compensation of persons covered by the plan. Although the credit does not apply after December 31, 1986, unused credit for contributions before that date still can be carried over. However, PAYSOP rules were also changed to permit distributions on plan terminations and to repeal the requirement that contributions had to be held in the plan for at least 84 months or the tax credits would be recaptured.
Violations of ERISA The examiner's review of employee benefit accounts sometimes reveals improper activity. Usually that activity violates either ERISA or 12 CFR 9.12, or both. In the latter case, the examiner should schedule the activity as a violation of both standards. However, in any case he/ she must make a recommendation for correction.
The examiner should request a reversal of the transaction at no loss to the account. The account should suffer neither loss of principal nor income from the original transaction or its reversal. The Department of Labor has stated that a reversal of such a transaction will not be considered a second prohibited transaction. The examiner is not limited to the option of reversal and may request that the bank obtain an exemption for the transaction from the Department of Labor.
ERISA Referrals to the Department of Labor The OCC and other members of the Federal Financial Institutions Examination Council agreed in 1980 to refer possible violations of the Employment Retirement Income Security Act (ERISA) to the Department of Labor (DOL).
Detailed information must be available for the OCC to determine whether an item falls under the agreement, and for the DOL to determine whether it wants to investigate the transaction. Therefore, when examiners learn of possible violations of Sections 404, 406, 407(a), 411 or 412 or ERISA during the supervisory process, a memorandum should be prepared. The memorandum should include the following information: • Name and location of the bank.
ERISA created the Pension Benefit Guaranty Corporation (PBGC) to ensure participants of plans covered by the termination insurance program and their beneficiaries against the loss of benefits arising from the complete or partial termination of their plan. The insurance of benefits is funded by premium payments made by plans covered by the program which include, with certain minor exceptions, all defined benefit plans. In addition to paying premiums to the PBGC, ERISA also imposed certain reporting and disclosure requirements on plans covered by the program.
For accounts where the bank acts as plan administrator, appropriate policies and procedures should be in place to comply with the reporting and disclosure provisions of ERISA.

TBC - Trust Banking Circular. TEC - Trust Examining Circular.

Corporate Trusts and Agencies Fiduciary Activities Corporations often require capital for extended periods of time and in amounts exceeding the lending ability of a single lender. Before the rise of large aggregations of savings through insurance companies and pension funds, no single buyer was able to buy a bond issue of substantial size. Bonds were therefore issued in denominations of $1,000 each and were sold to a large number of purchasers. The problem of communications between the numerous bondholders, and the impracticality of executing a note with each of the participating lenders gave rise to the corporate trustee to represent the bondholders and act as an intermediary between the borrower and lender. The trustee is presumed to act at all times for the protection of the bondholders and on their behalf. To this end, the trustee must ensure that the borrowing corporation complies with all the stipulations and provisions outlined within the corporate trust agreement. Although corporate departments provide a broad range of services, the trustee is not responsible for the purchase or underwriting of the securities or for finding buyers. Corporate trust accounts generally have a specific lifetime which terminates when the borrowing is repaid.
Types of Corporate Trusts The document creating the corporate trust usually is referred to as the agreement or indenture, and can be classified within three basic categories: • Mortgage trusts.
Mortgage Trust The mortgage trust refers to an issue which is secured by a lien on real property. The security or collateral may consist of real estate, buildings, and/or equipment. The trustee holds legal title to the property to ensure that bondholders or lenders have equal and/or proportionate security behind their investment. The examiner should ensure that the bank maintains adequate documentation to verify the priority of the lien against the collateral and, where required by the indenture, insurance policies or endorsements naming the bank, in its fiduciary capacity, as loss payee. If the collateral contains equipment, a security agreement and/or Uniform Commercial Code Financing Statement should be on file and updated as appropriate.
Collateral Trust The collateral trust agreement is similar to the mortgage trust except that the underlying security is not in the form of real property. The collateral can be in the form of securities, installment contracts, letters of credit, leasehold interests and revenues. The trust indenture may require that the collateral at all times exceed the value of the outstanding issue.
The examiner should determine that securities held as collateral are of sufficient amount and quality to meet specifications contained within the trust indenture.
Debenture Trust The third type of indenture, the unsecured form or debenture trust, has no collateral backing. The security behind this issue consists of the full faith and credit of the issuing entity. General obligation bonds issued by political subdivisions involve a common form of debenture trust. The ability to market such an issue is limited to well-established companies with high credit ratings.
The bank's appointment as trustee for unsecured trusteeships should be supported by a thorough financial analysis.
In addition to the broad categories of indentures described, there are many versions of corporate trusts being utilized to provide various financing alternatives.
One of the more complex areas of corporate trust administration involves the use of multiple trustees for leveraged lease financing. Due to the size and complexity generally associated with such financing, these projects often dictate a multiparty structure on both the equity side and the debt side of the transaction. To simplify documentation and to ensure the protection of the numerous parties involved, two trustees may be utilized; an owner trustee and an indenture trustee. The owner trustee holds legal title to the asset and represents the interest of the equity participants. The indenture trustee holds security interest in the property, collects rental payments from the lessee, and distributes payments due in accordance with debt agreements. As indicated above, the services provided by a bond trustee are varied. Therefore, the corporate indenture must define clearly all applicable duties and responsibilities of the trustee.
Responsibilities of the Bond Trustee The indenture or agreement which creates the trust is the primary authority for the definition of the duties and responsibilities of the trustee. The indenture should contain certain basic provisions which identify interested parties and specify the issuer's authority to execute transactions required to fulfill their duties and responsibilities. The indenture should define specifically the authority for the trustee to hold, sell or release property, the use of specific funds for the payment of principal and interest and procedures which the bank must follow in the event of default. The responsibilities of the bank in the event of default are particularly critical.
The bank can be held liable in the event that any subsequent loss to the bondholders is attributable to the trustee's negligence or failure to perform within the requirements of the indenture. The examiner must review carefully the trustee's system of internal controls to ensure that the bank is administering each account in accordance with provisions of the corporate indenture.
The examiner must also determine whether the bank has sufficient facilities and capabilities to fulfill properly all administrative requirements.

Corporate Trusts and Agencies Fiduciary Activities As noted previously, the examiner should verify that specific responsibilities contained in the indenture provisions are being followed by the trustee. These responsibilities may include short-term cash management or the establishment of one or more separate operating accounts. These accounts often include construction accounts from which builders' charges are to be paid, interest accounts, and sinking fund accounts.
The trustee is charged generally with specific duties in the event of default. Conditions that constitute default of an obligor should be defined clearly in the indenture. A default may occur at any time the obligor fails to meet any principal, interest, or sinking fund payments, files for bankruptcy or is declared bankrupt by the court. The trustee is under an obligation to represent the bondholders and in the event of default, it is advisable that the bank obtain opinion of counsel as to its duties, obligations, responsibilities and liabilities. The trustee may have to declare immediately the principal of the bonds due, take possession of collateral, and take all necessary steps to obtain a judgment against the obligor for all principal and accrued interest due. In addition, the indenture should define the actions to be taken by the trustee to maintain and/or monitor the collateral, which should include "drive by" inspections of the underlying property.
Essential Documentation In addition to the original agreement or corporate indenture, there are a number of essential items which should be maintained by the trustee in order to document proper account administration and supervision. These items are listed within the Compliance Handbook, Tier II, question 1. The documentation for each corporate trust account should include an opinion of counsel, which among other things, should state explicitly whether an issue requires approval of any state agency, or requires qualification and registration with the Securities and Exchange Commission.
Trust Indenture Act of 1939 Additional documentation is necessary for those securities which must register with the Securities and Exchange Commission under provisions of the Trust Indenture Act of 1939, an amendment to the Securities Act of 1933. The act resulted from conflict of interest dealings between issuers and trustees in pre-Depression days. Generally, all indentures providing for a total issuance exceeding $10 million principal value must be qualified. Exceptions to this rule are: • Issues of utilities and railroads.
Securities subject to the act must include certain provisions designed to aid the investing public. If these requirements are not met, securities may be disqualified resulting in the public sale being disallowed. Major items required by the Trust Indenture Act of 1939 include the filing of financial statements and other pertinent data of the obligor with the trustee and a requirement that if the trustee obtains any disqualifying interest, that interest must be cured within 90 days or the trustee must resign from the account. Under provisions of this act, the trustee is required to make an annual survey as of May 15 to determine that trust department holdings do not constitute a conflict-of-interest. The findings of this survey must be reported in a letter supplied to each bondholder. The examiner should find, on file, copies of this information, as well as any other reports that may be required from time to time by the SEC or the OCC.
Corporate Agencies The trustee of a bond issue may perform all duties relating to that issue, or agents may be appointed to perform one or more related functions. Some of the more common corporate agency capacities are: • Fiscal Agent. A fiscal agent generally performs the duties and responsibilities associated with a corporate trusteeship. This is often the case with state and municipal issues where the political subdivision acts as the implied trustee. The fiscal agent, however, is responsible only for those duties as required under an agency agreement rather than the encompassing responsibilities of a corporate fiduciary.
• Paying Agent. A paying agent for corporations and municipalities prepares and issues checks for the payment of interest, dividends, bonds redeemed, or matured. If the bonds are registered, the interest check is sent to the holder of record. For coupon or bearer bonds, payment is made upon presentation of the coupon. Ali paying agency accounts should be subject to periodic reconciliations. Examiners should review the bank's reconcilement activities and any reconciling items to ensure that procedures provide effective control.
• Dividend Disbursing Agent. A dividend disbursing agent performs similar functions as those provided by the paying agent for dividends on equity issues.
Dividends on stocks are sent directly to the shareholder of record. Examiners should review reconcilement procedures for dividend disbursement accounts as well as procedures for dividend checks returned from the post office to ensure that adequate control procedures are in place.
• Exchange Agent. An exchange agent receives securities from a company being acquired or subject to a capital reorganization and replaces them with stock or cash of the new or acquiring company.

Corporate Trusts and Agencies Fiduciary Activities • Depository. A depository acts in a corporate agency capacity in safekeeping customer securities. Most depositories facilitate securities trading by immobilizing the security and providing book-entry settlement between participants. A depository may provide dividend and interest collection services as well as a variety of other security-related functions.
There may be other less common agency functions being provided through the bank's corporate department. In each case, the examiner must be familiar with the provisions of the governing instrument to ensure that the bank's administration of the account is in conformance with stated requirements.
