TBC Trust Banking Circular. TEC - Trust Examining Circular.

Securityholder Services Fiduciary Activities Securityholder Servicing - General A transfer agent records changes in ownership and maintains accurate securityholder records. Although the term transfer agent is commonly associated with the transfer of shares of common stock, transfer agents can be utilized to record ownership and related transfer for any other type of security whose ownership is registered. Services rendered by transfer agents include, not only the transfer of registered securities, but also: • Preparation of shareholder lists.
• The mailing of stockholders notices and proxies.
Due to the growing complexity in the area of securityholder servicing, a bank acting in this capacity should be highly skilled and retain or employ legal counsel with expertise in this area.
Transfer agents are subject to strict regulatory requirements for the accuracy and timeliness of processing shareholder transactions. Given wide fluctuations in trading volume, transfer agents must be prepared to handle associated periods of peak transfer volume.
Due to the complexity of transfer agent responsibilities and liability to the bank which can result from inefficient or inaccurate shareholder processing, the examiner should ensure that written policies and procedures concerning all aspects of the bank's transfer operations are in place and that persons performing transfer functions are well trained.
Duties of the Transfer Agent The transfer agent records changes in ownership of a security by cancelling old certificates, updating shareholder files to record changes in ownership, and issuing new certificates. When a certificate is presented for transfer, the transfer agent must determine that the registered owner has assigned properly the ownership of the security presented for transfer and that the certificate is authentic. Procedures which should be followed by the bank to ascertain the validity of the transfer generally include a routine balancing of certificates received and examination of transfer documents to determine that: • The certificate has not been altered as to name, certificate number, or number of shares.
• It is a properly signed certificate of the issuer and it has been duly countersigned by the transfer agent and registrar.
• The assignment is dated, witnessed, and properly guaranteed.
All required supporting legal documents are in proper form.
• The name of the transferee is in a form acceptable to issue the new certificate.
• Federal and state taxes, if necessary, have been paid.
• There are no stop orders against the certificate.
Transfer agents must maintain a record of stop orders against lost and/or stolen securities or any other securities for which restrictive transfer provisions exist.
Additional review procedures are required for those items presented for transfer which require special documentation. Those items are referred to as legal transfers. This situation is encountered when the transferor is a corporation or anyone acting in a fiduciary or agency capacity. An person trained and qualified to review legal transfers often is needed to interpret legal documents, such as wills, trust instruments and/or powers of attorney to determine the validity of the transfer. To facilitate the transfer of securities, trustees often use nominee registration to avoid requirements of special legal review.
Once the transfer is properly authenticated and checked for any outstanding stop orders, shareholder files should be updated to reflect the change of ownership, cancellation of the old certificate, and issuance of a new certificate. Certificates presented for transfer should be cancelled as soon as possible to prevent the possibility of misappropriation. Once cancelled, those certificates should be safeguarded to prevent possible fraudulent use, and periodically, either returned to the company, or upon instructions from the company, destroyed.
In addition to updating shareholder records, the bank must obtain and update master securityholder files to include certain minimum certificate detail as required under Securities and Exchange Commission Rule 240.17Ad-10. Certificate detail should include, at a minimum: • The certificate number.
• The number of shares of equity securities or the principal dollar amount of debt securities represented by the certificate.
• The cancellation date of the certificate. In the case of mutual fund transfers which often involve the transfer of uncertificated securities, the examiner should ensure that shareholder file documentation includes all relevant account detail as well as an appropriate description of the transaction, i.e., purchase, redemption, transfer or exchange.

Securityholder Services Fiduciary Activities After shareholder files are updated to reflect the transfer of ownership, new certificates are prepared. In those cases in which the issuer has appointed a registrar, the new certificates along with cancelled debit certificates are sent to persons assigned responsibility for the registrar function.
Transfer entries are then balanced to ensure that shareholder records are in proof and that transfer activities do not create record differences.
Registrar Functions - General The primary duty of the registrar is to guard against overissuance of the stock or security. The registrar must maintain the control records representing aggregate authorized outstanding shares of an issue as well as records of all certificates issued and cancelled. Rules of the American Stock Exchange require the appointment of a registrar for all exchange traded issues.
The registrar acts as an additional check on the transfer agent to ensure that the performance of transfer activities does not result in an over-issuance of shares outstanding. Although in most cases, a bank is permitted to act in a dual capacity as both transfer agent and registrar of an issue, those functions must be performed independently.
Duties of a Registrar The registrar receives cancelled and newly issued certificates from the transfer agent. The registrar reviews the certificates to ensure that: The old certificates are genuine and have been properly cancelled.
• New and old certificates are of the same issue and the aggregate number of shares represented by the new certificates is exactly equal to the number of shares cancelled.
• In non-automated systems or when the bank acts as outside registrar with transfer functions performed by another entity, cancelled certificates are checked against outstanding stop notices.
• New certificates are issued in sequence with physical verification of the cancellation of any void or spoiled certificates.
In the case of certificates representing original issue or increase in outstanding shares, such increases are supported by written authorization of the issuing company and in the case of exchange traded issues, authorization of the exchange.
Upon completion of this review, the bank's signature as registrar is cancelled on the old certificate and new certificates are signed. The registrar journal is then updated and balanced as to certificates issued and cancelled.
Mutual Fund - Shareholder Services Mutual Fund - Transfer Corporate stock transfer agent functions, which involve normal certificate transfers, generally are characterized by equal or "one for one" transfers. The number of shares issued should equal the number of shares presented for transfer. Mutual fund transfer agent activities are significantly more complex because the funds are generally open-ended and involve constant fluctuation in outstanding shares and daily share price fluctuations. Outstanding share fluctuations are caused by a multitude of standard fund transactions including, but not limited to: • Direct purchase orders.
Exchanges and check withdrawal privileges.
Fluctuations in share price or net asset value result from changes in the market value of underlying securities held by a mutual fund. Ownership of the fund also may be represented by physical certificate or may be held in uncertificated or statement form.
Operational procedures, processing and reconcilement controls play a more critical role in mutual fund transfer agent operations. A bank acting as shareholder servicing agent often is responsible for receiving funds directly from customers, crediting the funds to shareholder accounts at the current price per share or net asset value and subsequent transfer of those funds to the mutual fund custodian. The bank also may redeem shares at current net asset value, debit shareholder records receive funds from the mutual fund custodian and remit proceeds to the shareholder.
Given the price dependent nature of mutual fund shareholder operations, failure by the bank to perform timely processing of shareholder transactions imposes additional liabilities than otherwise are present in corporate stock transfer processing. Items that are not processed on a timely basis must be backdated to ensure that the shareholder obtains the correct price per share. The bank as transfer agent may be liable for "buying in" any loss in processing backdated items that arises through significant price movement.
Mutual Fund Custodian The mutual fund custodian is responsible for the safekeeping and custody of securities that underlie the mutual fund. As in other custody account relationships, the banks' responsibilities should be clearly defined in the agreement and may include: Securityholder Services Fiduciary Activities • Collection of income.
• If specifically authorized, short term investment of uninvested cash.
The mutual fund custodian generally maintains a clearance account which is used to transfer the net proceeds of the funds daily purchase and redemption activity to or from the mutual fund custody account. Clearance account activity must be subject to daily reconciliation and review.
Fund Bookkeeper The fund bookkeeper maintains the control records for the fund's total shares outstanding as well as the investment portfolio records. The fund bookkeeper prices the underlying assets daily in accordance with procedures outlined within the fund prospectus.
With the exception of the additional responsibility for daily pricing, the bookkeeper serves a similar control function as that provided by the corporate stock registrar.
A bank may serve in one or more of the above capacities for a mutual fund. To ensure that the funds remain in proof, the bank should maintain evidence of reconciled positions between records of the shareholder transfer agent, custodian and fund bookkeeper. Examiners should review agreements appointing the bank as shareholder agent, custodian or fund bookkeeper as well as the fund prospectus, to ensure that the bank's responsibilities are clearly defined. Management and staffing levels also should be reviewed to determine that the bank is capable of providing the level of service outlined within the agreements on a prompt and reliable basis, with consideration of unanticipated volume surges often experienced by this industry.
Regulation 12 CFR 9.20 Agent Registration of Transfer When a national bank acts as transfer agent for any issuer with total assets exceeding $1 million and 500 or more equity securityholders of record, it is required to register with the Comptroller of the Currency as a transfer agent on Form TA-1. Issues for which the bank acts as transfer agent which trigger the registration requirement under 12 CFR 9.20 are those referred to as "covered securities." Covered securities are subject to registration with the SEC pursuant to Section 12 of the Securities Exchange Act of 1934.
Securities Exchange Act of 1934-SEC Rules 240.17Ad 1-7 Turnaround Performance and Recordkeeping Requirements for Registered Transfer Agents To facilitate the establishment of a national system for the clearance and settlement of securities transactions, the Securities and Exchange Commission adopted rules establishing minimum turnaround performance and recordkeeping requirements for registered transfer agents. The intent of those rules is to ensure that transfer agents: • Perform their transfer function in a prompt and accurate manner.
• Provide an early warning of inadequate transfer performance.
• Impose limitations on expansion of transfer agent activities when transfer agents fail to meet processing standards.
• Respond promptly to inquiries pertaining to items presented for transfer.
• Maintain and preserve certain records necessary to monitor compliance with those rules.
Transfer Agent Turnaround Performance Requirements To ensure that transfer agents perform their transfer functions in a prompt and accurate manner, the turnaround rules require the agent to process at least 90 percent of all routine items presented for transfer within three business days. To compute accurate turnaround, items received before noon are considered to be received at noon. Items received after noon on a business day or on a nonbusiness day are considered to be received at noon on the next business day. Routine items are classified as those items for which: There is no stop transfer notice on file, an adverse claim or transfer restrictions (note: transfer restrictions generally apply to those securities subject to Rule 144 of the Securities and Exchange Commission). This rule sets forth certain conditions which must be met prior to transfer of ownership of restricted securities. Restricted securities are generally defined as those securities: For which adequate current information is not available to the public.
- Owned by persons controlling, controlled by or under common control with the issuer. The transfer agent should follow specific procedures regarding the transfer of securities subject to SEC Rule 144.
• No additional documentation, instruction or opinion of counsel is necessary before the transfer can be effected.
• The only attached documentation requiring review is: - The assignment, endorsement or stock power.
- Certain corporate resolutions.
Signature guarantees.
- Tax payment or waiver certificates.

Securityholder Services Fiduciary Activities • The transfer is not in connection with a reorganization, tender offer, exchange, redemption or liquidation, exercise or conversion.
Even though the item may be classified as a legal item because certain legal documentation may have to be reviewed, this will not necessarily designate the item as nonroutine.
Registrar Function Turnaround Performance Registrars which perform only the registrar function and not the transfer of an issue, are defined as outside registrars. Every outside registrar must process at least 90 percent of all routine items received during a month on the next business day.
Failure to Meet Performance Standards Regulatory Filing Transfer agents who fail to meet the transfer agent or outside registrar turnaround performance requirements for any one month must, within 10 business days following the end of the month, file with the Comptroller of the Currency and the SEC notice of such failure. The specific information required is defined under Section 17Ad-2.
Limitation on Expansion Transfer agents or outside registrars which fail for three consecutive months to meet turnaround requirements are subject to a three-month limitation on expansion of business activities. In addition, the same limitation on expansion applies to any transfer agent or registrar who for two consecutive months, fails to turnaround at least 75 percent of the routine items received.
Exemption from Turnaround Requirements A registered transfer agent receiving fewer than 500 items for transfer or fewer than 500 items for processing as a registrar within six consecutive months is considered an exempt transfer agent. An exemption notice must be prepared and maintained by the transfer agent.
An exempt transfer agent is required to calculate, on a monthly basis, the number of items received for transfer or processing. Whenever any exempt transfer agent receives more than 500 items during any consecutive six-month period, it ceases to qualify for exemption and must prepare and maintain in its possession a document stating that fact. The Comptroller of the Currency also can exempt a transfer agent from any requirements of the act, including turnaround, if the exemption is in the public interest and would be consistent with the purposes of the act. Those appeals would be appropriate under limited circumstances and only when the failure to meet required standards was totally beyond the control of the transfer agent. The turnaround rules also would not apply to transfer agents engaged exclusively in the transfer of open-ended redeemable securities of investment companies. As noted previously, transfer of those shares is significantly more complex than the transer of ownership of stock and bonds and is subject to standards imposed under the Investment Company Act of 1940.
Written Inquiries Section 17Ad-5 establishes certain time frames in which the transfer agent is required to respond to written requests concerning the status of items presented for transfer. The transfer agent is required to respond: • Within 5 business days for inquiries concerning the status of items presented for transfer.
• Within 5 business days when a broker dealer requests, in writing, acknowledgment of transfer instructions or revalidation of a window ticket.
• Within 10 business days to requests for confirmation of certificate possession as of a given date during the preceding 30 days.
• Within 20 business days to requests for an account transcript.
• Within 10 business days to current claims or 20 business days to aged claims concerning dividend and interest payments when such claims contain sufficient detail.
Recordkeeping Requirements Transfer agents are required to maintain a book, schedule, log, or other record for the number of routine and nonroutine items received by the transfer agent or registrar during a given month. Although the rules do not specify a required format, records maintained by the transfer agent must be of sufficient detail to allow the transfer agent to monitor and keep adequate control over its own performance as well as permit examiners to examine registered transfer agents on a historical basis for compliance with applicable rules.
Transfer agents are also required to maintain detailed records on all written inquiries and responses to those inquiries. This record should include the number of written inquiries not responded to within required time frames.
Rule 496 New York Stock Exchange/Rule 891 American Stock Exchange Rule 496 of the New York Stock Exchange and Rule 891 of the American Stock Exchange define specific requirements for transfer agents operating outside of New York City. Those rules impose a 48-hour turnaround requirement on transfer of securities listed on the New York Stock Exchange and the American Stock Exchange. The Securities and Exchange Commission has ruled that the three-day turnaround requirements resecurityholder Services Fiduciary Activities quired under Section 240.17Ad-2 are not intended to, and do not supersede any exchange rules which may impose more stringent standards.
Securities Exchange Act of 1934-SEC Rule 17f-1Lost and Stolen Securities Program The Securities and Exchange Commission adopted the lost and stolen securities program in response to the increasing growth in securities theft and counterfeiting activity. This rule requires that reporting institutions, which include all banks and registered transfer agents, report or make inquiries about any missing, lost, counterfeit or stolen securities. The Securities Information Center (SIC) has been delegated the responsibility for the operational and reporting aspects of this rule by the Securities and Exchange Commission.
Inquiry Requirements All reporting institutions must make formal inquiries to the SIC, for all securities coming into their possession, to determine if they have been reported lost, stolen, or missing. No inquiry is required, however, if the security was received from: • Another reporting institution.
• A customer of the reporting institution and it is registered in the name of the customer or customer's nominee.
Reporting Requirements All reporting institutions must report to the SIC on Form X-17F-1A any incidence of missing, lost or stolen securities. Those reports should be made upon the discovery of: • A theft of securities.
• Securities lost or missing as a result of audit counts, no later than 10 days after the completion of the audit.
• Any other identifying information about securities.
• Any other incidence of missing or stolen securities.
Securities Exchange Act of 1934-SEC Rule 17f-2— Fingerprinting Requirements Rule 17f-2 requires that banks acting as registered transfer agents fingerprint any director, officer or employee who has access to or handles any securities, records or monies relating to transfer agent functions, or has direct supervisory responsibility over such persons. Those fingerprints must be submitted to the Attorney General of the United States for identification and processing. All other classes of bank employees may be exempted from fingerprinting requirements if a Notice Pursuant to Rule 17f-2 is prepared, kept current, and located in an easily accessible place in the principal office of the transfer agent and in the office employing the exempt persons. If no persons meet the requirements for exemption from fingerprinting or if the bank elects to fingerprint all employees, a notice to that effect must be filed.
Many transfer agents may employ temporary employees in periods of peak volume transfers. Examiners should ensure that the bank has a policy of fingerprinting those employees subject to the requirements of Rule 17f-2.
17 CFR 240.17Ad 9-13 Maintenance of Accurate Securityholder Files and Safeguarding of Funds and Securities by Registered Transfer Agents Minimum Information in Master Securityholder File The "master security file," which is the official list of individual securityholder accounts, must contain the following minimum certificate detail: • Certificate number (not applicable to uncertificated securities).
• The number of shares for equity securities or the principal dollar amount for debt securities.
• For redeemable securities of investment companies, an appropriate description of each debit and credit (i.e., designation indicating purchase, redemption, or transfer).
• Any other identifying information about securities or securityholders that the transfer agent reasonably deems essential to its recordkeeping system for the efficient and effective research of record differences.
Prompt Posting of Master Securityholder File Every recordkeeping transfer agent (the transfer agent that maintains and updates the master securityholder file) must promptly and accurately post to the master securityholder file debits and credits containing minimum and appropriate certificate detail which represents every security transferred, purchased, redeemed or issued; provided, however, that if a security transferred or redeemed contains certificate detail different from that currently posted to the master securityholder file, the credit shall be posted to the master securityholder file and the debit and related certificate detail shall be maintained in a subsidiary file until resolved.

Securityholder Services Fiduciary Activities • For recordkeeping transfer agents that (1) perform transfer agent functions solely for their own or affiliated companies' securities issues, and (2) employ batch posting systems, 10 business days.
• For all other recordkeeping transfer agents, five business days.
When there is a co-transfer agent, the time frame for prompt posting begins when the recordkeeping transfer agent receives the co-transfer agent's transfer journals.
Maintenance of Accurate Securityholder Files and "Buyin" of Physical Overissuance Recordkeeping transfer agents must exercise diligent and continuous attention to resolving record differences. A "record difference" occurs when either: (1) The total number of shares or total principal dollar amount of securities in the master securityholder file does not equal the number of shares or principal dollar amount authorized and issued; or (2) The security transferred or redeemed contains certificate detail different from the certificate detail currently on the master securityholder file, which difference cannot be immediately resolved. The master securityholder file and subsidiary files must represent accurately all relevant debits and credits until the record difference is resolved. If a record difference is an actual physical overissuance, the transfer agent that erroneously issued the certificate(s) giving rise to the overissuance shall, within 60 days of the discovery of such overissuance, buy-in securities equal to either the number of shares in the case of equity securities or the principal dollar amount in the case of debt securities. A 30-day extension is allowed if the transfer agent obtains a letter from the party holding the overissued certificates confirming that the certificates will be returned to the transfer agent not later than 30 days after the end of the 60-day period. If the certificates are not returned by the end of this additional time, the transfer agent must execute the buy-in.
Reports to Regulatory Agencies Recordkeeping transfer agents must report the information specified in Rule 17Ad-11(d)(1) within 10 business days following the end of each calendar quarter when the aggregate market value of record differences for all issues for which it performs recordkeeping functions exceeds the thresholds specified in Rule 17Ad11(c)(1).
Within 10 business days following the end of each calendar quarter, every recordkeeping transfer agent shall report the information specified in Rule 17Ad11(d)(2) to its appropriate regulatory agency concerning buy-ins of all issues for which it acts as recordkeeping transfer agent when the aggregate market value of all buy-ins executed pursuant to 17Ad-10(g) during that quarter exceeds $100,000.
Rule 17Ad-22 requires all registered transfer agents to file an annual report on Form TA-2 with the SEC by August 31 of each year except as follows: • Transfer agents that receive fewer than 500 items for transfer and fewer than 500 items for processing in the 6 months ending June 30 of each year, and do not maintain more than 1,000 individual securityholder accounts as of June 30 are only required to complete items one through four and the execution of Form TA-2.
• Transfer agents do not have to file Form TA-2 if they retain a service company to perform all of their transfer agent functions. However, the service company must file Form TA-2. (Meinhard v. Salmon, 249 N.Y. 458, 164 N.E. 545, 546, 1928) This statement concisely reflects the standard to which fiduciaries are held even today by courts of equity. The standard to which national bank fiduciaries must adhere is codified further in 12 CFR 9.12.
Conflicts of interest and self dealing transactions technically refer to separate activities depending on whether the corporate fiduciary deals with itself or a related party; however for this discussion the terms are treated interchangeably. Courts traditionally have treated either type of transaction, when not conducted on an "arms-length" basis, as voidable pursuant to beneficiary suit.
Authority for Conflicts of Interest Transactions As reflected in the provisions of 12 CFR 9.12 and precedents of this Office, conflict transactions may be authorized by specific provisions in the governing instrument, local law, or appropriate order of court. In the absence of such authorization, the trustee may enter into self dealing or conflict transactions with the proper consent of all beneficiaries, provided the transaction is fair and in the best interest of the beneficiaries. Proper consent, as used here, means full and complete disclosure of the facts surrounding the conflict and knowledgeable consent of the beneficiary. For the trustee to obtain such consent from all beneficiaries can be difficult. There may be more than one class of contingent remaindermen. Some beneficiaries may be minors, and some may be as yet unborn. Guardians would have to be appointed for the minors and unborn beneficiaries.
All must be represented before the court and an order obtained approving the transaction.
A settlor who reserves an absolute power of modification or revocation over a trust instrument is considered, for most purposes, the absolute owner of the trust res.
As a result, the settlor reserving such power may authorize self dealing or conflicts of interest transactions on the part of the fiduciary. In those instances, however, the fiduciary should seek protection through appropriate amendments to the governing instrument.
A breach of the duty of undivided loyalty by the fiduciary cannot be excused on the basis that the governing instrument gives broad authority and discretion in the administration of the trust. To effectively waive the rule of undivided loyalty, authority in the governing instrument must be specific as to the nature of the conflict.
Examinations During an examination of fiduciary activities, the examiner should be aware of the potential for conflict transactions by the trustee. Situations in which the examiner is more likely to find evidence of self dealing or conflicts of interest include: • Investment in or retention of shares of stock or obligations of the bank, holding company, affiliates, or other related organizations.
• Deposit of fiduciary funds in the commercial bank or related banks.
• Purchases of securities for fiduciary accounts from syndicates in which the bank has participated.
• Use of affiliated discount brokerage companies.
• Voting of bank stock or holding company stock.
Own Bank Securities/Obligations The investment in or retention of shares of own bank stock places the trustee in a conflicting position where it cannot determine whether to sell or retain shares of the trustee bank with the same objectivity with which it could approach the purchase, sale or retention of other securities. A corporate trustee cannot purchase shares of its own stock unless properly authorized by the terms of the governing instrument, court order or local law (see 12 CFR 9.12(a)). This is true even if the purchase is otherwise a proper trust investment. The fact that the governing instrument grants the trustee broad investment powers would not be sufficient authorization for the purchase of own bank stock.
Unless retention is authorized by the terms of the trust or by local law, the trustee is under a duty to dispose of Conflicts of Interest Fiduciary Activities securities received in kind which are not proper trust investments. When the shares are those of the trustee bank, the fact that the trustee has knowledge of the value of the shares and the state of its business should be considered in determining whether retention is appropriate. Even when retention would otherwise be appropriate, it has been held that the trustee is under a duty to dispose of its own shares if retention is not authorized by the terms of the governing instrument or local law. Neither does it prohibit the integration of joint marketing and servicing of fiduciary and commercial department customers. The original and continuing intent of the Chinese Wall was to prevent the passage of material inside information between a bank's fiduciary and commercial departments in violation of securities laws and regulations.
With the enactment of the Insider Trading and Securities Fraud Enforcement Act of 1988 (the act) penalties for insider trading and use of material information increased to a maximum of $1,000,000 and 10 years imprisonment. The penalties may be assessed on the bank if one of its employees engages in insider trading, the individual employee, and even the manager of the employee. In addition the act subjects banks and management level employees to potential liability if they fail to take appropriate steps to prevent insider trading.
Although the act does not detail specific steps to be taken, it is expected that policies and procedures appropriate to the restriction of the use of nonpublic information would be adopted.

Conflicts of Interest Fiduciary Activities Syndications The duty of undivided loyalty also prohibits the trustee from purchasing for its fiduciary accounts, securities.
that are being underwritten by the commercial department of the bank, either individually or as a member of a syndicate. This would normally preclude a corporate trustee from purchasing securities from the commercial department when the bank is a member of a divided or undivided syndicate. It would also preclude the purchase of securities from an affiliate of the corporate fiduciary when the affiliate is a member of a divided or undivided syndicate, even though the corporate fiduciary is not a member.7376, is a corporation of which at least 80 percent of the voting stock is owned by a bank which performs a business function which the parent bank is legally authorized to perform. Such a subsidiary also must be included as part of the supervisory activity when it is engaged in fiduciary related activities, unless it primarily provides services to nonaffiliated trust departments (see "Outside Service Corporations"). The separate corporate existence of a nonbank affiliate provides, within limits, a legal screen between the bank and its affiliate. However, difficulties with a holding company or its nonbank subsidiary can be detrimental to the bank and, therefore, its condition and operations must be carefully reviewed to determine its effect on the bank's condition.
Some banks have separated their investment activities from the bank by creating an investment advisory subsidiary. Some have been formed as subsidiaries of the bank holding company. Those approaches are implemented largely to improve compensation for their investment staff or to create the impression through a name change that their investment organizations are not banks or bank-affiliated. In addition, some banks have acquired investment advisory companies. In all cases, those companies are related organizations engaged in fiduciary activities and subject to OCC review.
The fundamental objective of such review is to ensure that the activities of the investment advisory affiliate or subsidiary are being executed in a manner not detrimental to the affairs or soundness of the bank. This is accomplished by ensuring that the interests of the beneficiaries and other parties at interest are protected through compliance with applicable statutes, rules, regulations and sound internal controls. Examiners must remain aware that when the senior investment professional, or a group of them, resign it may not significantly affect parent bank or holding company revenues because of the methods of compensation.
However, investment affiliates are highly visible units.
Prospective clients tend to shy away from both the parent and the affiliate when there are changes in senior staff. This can also affect the bank's lending and trust operations through the severance of established customer relationships.
To the extent applicable, the procedures outlined in the Fiduciary Activities section of the Comptroller's Handbook for Compliance should be employed in examining the policies, practices and controls of the investment advisory company, including the program of internal and external audit. In addition, the examiner should determine that the company business is being conducted in conformity with sound fiduciary and business practices and ascertain that it is financially sound. In evaluating financial soundness, the examiner should: • For capitalization of the company, Assess the adequacy of operating capital.
- Determine the extent to which the company must borrow for operating expenses.
-Determine the extent of financial dependence on the holding company and/or the bank.
• For earnings, - Determine whether services are adequately priced to cover costs and promote profitability.
- Ascertain that fees paid from fiduciary activities to the company are reasonable and in proportion to service rendered. (See Banking Circular 115.) Review income and expense trends.
• For assets/liabilities, Determine whether ratio of current assets to current liabilities is acceptable.
Assess ratio of debt to net worth.
• For insurance, - Determine whether directors, officers and employees are adequately bonded.
- Determine whether the company is protected adequately against all other hazards as are covered customarily by insurance.
The examiner can obtain pertinent information regarding the relationship between the bank, its holding company and the related organization by reviewing the holding company's annual report on Form 10-K and quarterly report on Form 10-Q. Those reports, which contain financial statements and narrative information, are required to be filed with the Securities and Exchange Commission pursuant to Section 13 or 15(d) of the Securities Exchange Act of 1934. Copies of the reports must be kept at the main office of the bank for review by examiners.
The review of investment advisory affiliates and subsidiaries is considered an extension of the fiduciary supergeneral Trust Supervision Matters Fiduciary Activities visory activity and, as such, should be included as part of the Report of Supervisory Activity (ROSA). Time spent in the examination of the investment advisory affiliate must be included in the bill for the fiduciary supervisory activity. Charges will be based on the rates currently in effect. Other types of related organizations of a national bank which perform fiduciary functions or provide fiduciary services for the bank also are subject to OCC examinations. The decision to examine other related organizations which the examiner might encounter will be made by the district deputy comptroller after consultation with the Compliance Management Division in Washington.
Outside Service Corporations Pursuant to 12 U.S.C. 1865, any services performed for a national bank by an outside servicer shall be subject to OCC regulation and examination to the same extent as if the services were performed by the bank itself. In recent years, an increasing number of corporations have been providing support services, such as electronic data processing or stock transfer, to corporate trustees on a nationwide basis. Although some of those organizations are affiliated with banks and bank holding companies, others are independent. In addition, some companies that are bank affiliated provide substantial servicing to nonaffiliated entities. Therefore, those organizations are examined independently by the OCC and a separate ROSA and bill for special services are prepared.
The ROSA and bill should be forwarded to the field or district office where they should be reviewed for accuracy and completeness. The original typed copy of the ROSA is forwarded by the district office to the Washington office pursuant to existing policy. Copies also should be retained in the district and/or field office files. The field office should pursue all criticisms and direct copies of correspondence relating to matters of national significance to the Compliance Management Division in Washington. Normally, the OCC will not distribute such ROSAS to banks or organizations using an outside service corporation. However, a copy of the ROSA will be furnished to the service organization for its confidential use and may be provided to users at the organization's discretion.
Time spent in the examination will be billed to the outside service corporation based on the rates currently in effect for fiduciary supervisory activities.
Enforcement Policy The authority to initiate, negotiate, execute, modify or terminate enforcement actions involving banks supervised by the districts is delegated to the district deputy comptroller or to the deputy comptroller for Special Supervision for nondelegated banks.
With respect to enforcement actions relating to problems in fiduciary activities, the district deputy comptroller or the deputy comptroller for Special Supervision shall consult with the Compliance Management Division in the Washington office (see PPM 5310-3, Article V).
Continuous Monitoring of Fiduciary Activities or Fiduciary Portfolio Management A designated goal of the OCC is to continue implementing the supervisory approach, including bank portfolio management. These goals are outlined in the bank supervision policy set forth in PPM 5000-20 and Supplement 1. Efforts toward these goals should include performing continuous monitoring of the fiduciary activities areas within the banks. The portfolio manager must maintain current information regarding fiduciary activities and use the information to tailor and update the supervisory strategy for the fiduciary activities area.
Often the portfolio manager must rely on examiners with high levels of fiduciary experience to provide assistance in the continuous monitoring of the fiduciary activities area of the bank.
Compliance Procedures The development of the Compliance Program in April 1987 shifted the examination emphasis from a review of individual transactions to an assessment of the bank's systems for ensuring compliance with laws and regulations. Fiduciary activities are included in the Compliance Program.
The procedures for the fiduciary area were deleted from the Comptroller's Handbook for National Trust Examiners and are now included in the Comptroller's Handbook for Compliance. The procedures are substantially the same procedures as used in the past; however, they are not arranged into different levels or "tiers." Tier I procedures represent the minimum review needed to determine the adequacy of the bank's compliance systems. Tier II procedures are more detailed in coverage and should only be used if the examiner is unable to reach a conclusion in a particular area or if Tier I procedures disclose significant violations and/or inadequate compliance systems. Tier II procedures are also useful for training examiners with less experience in a specific area. For further details on the Compliance Program and the procedures, please refer to the Comptroller's Handbook for Compliance.
Requests for Ruling When supervisory activities uncover matters which properly involve an interpretation of Office policy or the establishment of national policy on fiduciary subjects or legal issues, they should be processed for action as requests for ruling. Ruling requests on fiduciary subjects should be set forth in a memorandum addressed to the district counsel who will either resolve the request or forward it to the Compliance Management Division.
Reports of supervisory activity should indicate that a request for ruling has been made.

General Trust Supervision Matters Fiduciary Activities Consumer Compliance The activities of a corporate fiduciary are subject to compliance with consumer laws unless specifically exempted. For example, an individual trust which extends credit more than 25 times, or 5 times for transactions secured by residential dwellings, during a calendar year is considered a creditor within the meaning of 12 CFR 226, Regulation Z. The trust is, therefore, subject to the disclosure requirements, and the responsibility for such disclosure falls on the bank as trustee.
On the other hand, loans originated or purchased by the bank acting as a trustee or in some other fiduciary capacity are specifically omitted from the disclosure.
statements required by the Home Mortgage Disclosure Act, 12 CFR 203.
The review of fiduciary activities for compliance with consumer laws is part of the consumer supervisory activity process. The examiner assigned the review of fiduciary activities should coordinate with the examiner assigned the consumer compliance review to ensure appropriate coverage. (See section 506.500.1 of the Comptroller's Handbook of Consumer Examinations for a listing of consumer laws applicable to fiduciary activities) Requests for Fiduciary Powers A national bank desiring to exercise fiduciary powers must submit a letter of intent to the appropriate district deputy comptroller pursuant to 12 CFR 5.26. State banks which convert to national banks must also submit this letter if they plan to exercise fiduciary powers. The format and content of the letter, as well as supporting instructions, may be furnished by the district deputy comptroller upon request. The factors considered by the Comptroller of the Currency in acting upon requests to exercise fiduciary powers are set forth in 12 CFR 5.26.
Unless the time frame for reviewing the bank's request is extended, approval to exercise the requested powers will be effective on or before the 30th day after the filing is received by this Office. Under appropriate circumstances, the Office reserves the right to schedule an opening investigation.
Surrender of Fiduciary Powers If a national bank desires to discontinue or terminate its fiduciary services, the board of directors must pass a resolution so signifying, and file it with the appropriate district office. The bank may be classified either as inactive or it may surrender its fiduciary powers altogether. In either case, the board of directors must arrange for a final audit of the fiduciary accounts. In addition, the OCC may conduct a closing investigation to determine if the bank has been completely discharged from its fiduciary obligations, i.e., that all accounts have been properly closed and distributed. The decision to conduct a closing investigation will be made by the district office. Those banks which surrender fiduciary powers entirely must forward their original certification document to the district office for cancellation. A bank which surrenders its fiduciary powers will receive a certification from the district office pursuant to 12 U.S.C. 92a(j) indicating that it no longer has the authority to exercise fiduciary powers.

International Fiduciary Activities International fiduciary banking is a relatively recent service offered by U.S. banks. The primary areas of business, investment advisory and custodial services are sometimes related to merchant banking activities.
Multinationalism, the interdependency of national economies, and various legal factors increasingly are making international fiduciary services more desirable for bank customers. The growing integration of world securities markets has facilitated and, in some circumstances, mandated global investment and portfolio diversification strategies. Foreign and domestic customers require a source of investment services and legal expertise to take advantage of appropriate international investment opportunities. As service institutions, national banks provide such assistance through global networks, correspondent relationships, foreign branches, and foreign subsidiaries. Those facilities allow banks to offer fiduciary services to foreign citizens and overseas affiliates of U.S. corporations, while also facilitating international investment and custodial services for domestic accounts.
Multinational banks are the primary providers of such services, although some regional banks also are engaged in those activities. The banks can be divided into two categories: (1) banks which have accounts administered in overseas branches and/or affiliates; and (2) banks which do not administer accounts in their offices overseas, but invest internationally for their domestic customers. Those banks are examined in accordance with multinational division or district office priorities, with the assistance of the trust examinations division.
Law and Type of Entity International fiduciary business may be conducted by an overseas branch of a national bank. In this case, 12 CFR 9 is applicable through the provisions of 12 U.S.C.
481 and 12 U.S.C. 1818(b)(1). International fiduciary business may also be conducted by an Edge Act Corporation or a subsidiary of the parent holding company. In that case, Regulation K of the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System (i.e., 12 CFR 211) applies. 12 CFR 9 is used normally as a professional guideline in these instances, although it could be applied indirectly by reason of 12 U.S.C. 1867, if the affiliate provides fiduciary services to a branch. Other U.S. laws and regulations may or may not be applicable, depending upon specific provisions and exemptions. Foreign branches of U.S. banks, for example, are exempt from the provisions of 12 CFR 12 and are not covered under FDIC insurance. All types of foreign entities, on the other hand, can be included under various provisions of the Foreign Corrupt Practices Act of 1977.
Authority to examine books and records of such overseas units is contained in 12 CFR 9.8 and/or 12 CFR 211.7, and is based on the various sections of Title 12 of the United States Code.
Situs of Account/Citizenship of Customers Situs of accounts and the citizenship of customers also help to determine the applicability of 12 CFR 9 and other laws and regulations, both U.S. and foreign. International aspects of U.S. based accounts, such as investments and operations, are covered under 12 CFR 9 and various U.S. laws, particularly the Employee Retirement Income Security Act of 1974 and the Trust Indenture Act of 1939. Foreign based accounts may be covered by 12 CFR 9 (depending on type of entity) and are included under applicable foreign laws, particularly those related to professional secrecy, contract law and fiduciary standards.
Examination Planning and Control Careful planning, coordination, and control are as important in international examinations as they are in domestic ones. The approaches and practices outlined in this section generally are applicable. However, international fiduciary activities usually will be examined in conjunction with the domestic examination of both commercial and fiduciary activities. The examination therefore also may require the employment and adaptation of the general trust examination procedures contained in this handbook. Modifications may be made as appropriate to examine the specific activities of an international unit and/or various international aspects of domestic account administration. A high degree of flexibility and professional judgment must be applied to meet examination objectives and to observe various secrecy laws and limitations on physical observation and contact.
The selection of international fiduciary activity to be examined is made by the supervisory office based on data accumulated through the Annual Report of International Fiduciary Activities (FFIEC-006). Scheduling and assignment of personnel are suggested to either Multinational Banking in the Washington office, or the appropriate district office for district examinations.
Administrative arrangements for overseas assignments are made through Multinational Banking. Additional coordination with other government agencies may be required and will be effected as necessary. The supervisory office ascertains whether the international examinations will be performed domestically or overseas. The international trust examiner-in-charge suggests the scope of the examination and issues a questionnaire to the bank requesting necessary data. If necessary, clearance by foreign governments and authorities will be obtained to gain access to fiduciary books and records. Examination data is assembled and delivered by the bank to the designated location, either domestic or foreign. When requested by the bank, arrangements may be coordinated with a bank liaison official. This official may be present during the examination if books and records are subject to secrecy restrictions. The international trust examiner-in-charge gathers and transinternational Fiduciary Activities ports the necessary information, such as previous examination reports, relevant correspondence and other pertinent documents, to the site of the international trust examination.
The international trust examiner-in-charge should, whenever possible, perform a pre-examination analysis at the domestic bank to acquire supplementary information that may expedite the examination. If the international examiner cannot perform the analysis, it should be conducted by the domestic trust examiner-in-charge who provides the international trust examiner-in-charge with relevant data. Such data may include information contained in the minutes of domestic boards and committees, international economic and investment research reports, or operational and audit reports on Overseas units which may be directed to the parent holding company or bank.
The international trust examiner-in-charge of an overseas examination is responsible for communicating relevant information, such as location and starting time of the examination, to assisting personnel and any designated bank liaison official. Exit reviews of international fiduciary examinations should be conducted with senior management of both the international unit and the parent/lead bank. Meetings should be held with both the domestic trust examiner-in-charge and the commercial examiner-in-charge of the consolidated bank examination.
Statistical Sampling Judgmental sampling techniques are normally utilized in international fiduciary examinations. Accounts to be examined from secrecy law jurisdictions are preselected from a coded, sequentially numbered list provided by the bank prior to the examination. Relevant information on the preselected accounts is then made available by the bank to the international trust examinerin-charge. In nonsecrecy law jurisdictions, the examiner makes his/her own selection of accounts to be examined at the inception of the on-premise examination.
Working Papers Working papers should consist of written documentation of the examination procedures followed and the conclusions reached during the examination. Data made available to the examiner by the bank in response to the OCC questionnaire also should be retained as part of the examination working papers.
The definition, purpose, quality standards, preparation and organization of working papers are the same as those discussed elsewhere in the handbook. The international trust examiner-in-charge is responsible for forwarding appropriate work papers through the U. S.
Embassy or Consulate to the OCC supervisory office.
Working papers for international examinations conducted domestically must be maintained in the bank, under seal, or in the local OCC office, pursuant to current district policy.
Examination of the varied types of international fiduciary activity require reference to and use of other sections of this handbook. Guidelines for using those sections in an examination are presented below under the title of each handbook section.
Management Appraisal The overall evaluation of the international fiduciary division and/or unit should be made by the international trust examiner-in-charge. The examiner, in completing the applicable parts of this program, must be fully cognizant of the complexities of a large international bank. The reporting structure can be complex in those cases where various domestic and international services have been realigned with complementary areas of commercial banking. Any matrix reporting structure must be fully understood. Proper management supervision of international fiduciary activities is necessary to effect compliance with the applicable laws and sound management principles. An integrated policy manual and proper expertise of management are two means by which such compliance may be achieved.
Management of international fiduciary activities requires specific expertise, in addition to the knowledge needed for the management of domestic accounts and investments. International managers should be knowledgeable in: Applicable foreign and U.S. tax and property laws to ensure proper estate planning and distribution.
• Various foreign fiduciary, secrecy, and other laws to ensure proper account administration.
• Foreign customs, operational practices, markets, foreign exchange, and investments to perform an effective level of fiduciary service.
Management should be evaluated in context of both the local and the overall environment and policies of the bank.
Operations, Controls and Audits The operational aspects of fiduciary activities abroad involve practices that can be significantly different from domestic trust operations. Securities trading and clearing practices vary from country to country, and commonly involve utilization of several subcustodians. Dividend and interest payments usually are made less frequently than quarterly. Other corporate actions may not be disclosed to the extent required by comparable U.S. law. Basic internal controls should be observed in foreign accounting systems. In some cases, the extent of foreign exchange or other activities may necessitate additional controls. In completing the applicable parts of this work program, the examiner should be aware of local operational practices and judge the adequacy of International Fiduciary Activities related controls accordingly. However, inadequate systems cannot be justified on the basis that they conform to local practice.
Insurance considerations and pledge requirements in sub-custodial banks and other financial institutions should be reviewed in both the local and overall contexts. Some foreign laws require a pledge of assets in order to conduct fiduciary and related activities. The calculation of the pledge requirements of 12 CFR 9.10(b) should include consideration of the fact that FDIC insurance coverage is not present in foreign branches of U.S. banks. The extent to which specific investment authorization has been obtained is as relevant here as in domestic operations. As discussed earlier, whether those regulatory requirements are strictly applicable to the foreign operation should be determined.
The examiner should evaluate the internal and external audit programs in light of OCC guidelines for the adequacy of trust department audit. The scope of the audit program and audit techniques may differ from typical domestic approaches because of the differences in international operational and administrative procedures.
Each overseas unit may be subject to a separate audit program. Copies of core audit programs, the latest audit reports, and the related management responses should be retained with the working papers.
Asset Administration Discretionary account investments should conform generally to the same basic standards of prudence and diversification as do domestic investments in the absence of specific language to the contrary in the governing instrument. The concepts embodied in related U.S. case law usually are recognized as general standards in common law jurisdictions and may be duplicated, to some extent, by statute in civil law jurisdictions. When utilizing this work program to determine investment quality, the examiner should rely primarily on the bank's research and approved list because investment service ratings frequently are not available.
International investments are subject essentially to the same risks as are international loans, albeit occasionally more indirectly. The nature of this risk is more complex because of the multiplicity of jurisdictions and currencies which may be represented in an internationally diversified portfolio of securities. The assessment of international risks, particularly country and foreign exchange risk, is essential in the investment review process. Documentation of economic and foreign currency reviews must be included in examination testing.
Foreign investment portfolios often are similar in type to domestic ones but may have different characteristics, such as frequency of income payments. Similarly, the financial statements will reflect the variety of accounting practices used in different countries. Foreign short term investments are generally more elementary in nature than in the U.S. Typical investments include interestbearing call deposits (which are similar to demand deposits), negotiable certificates of deposit and other time deposits. Other investments may include unit trusts, which are similar to collective investment funds or mutual funds, and bankers acceptances. Precious metals, particularly gold and silver coins and bullion, are included in many portfolios as a result of local investment preferences.
Account Administration Trusts exist in all common law jurisdictions. The provisions of foreign trust instruments reflect the enabling statutes or practices of the particular jurisdiction and are similar to those contained in domestic instruments.
The work programs related to this area can be applied directly, although different terminology and special "flee" clauses may be found. Civil law jurisdictions generally do not recognize the fiduciary concept as it is known in common law countries, except by enabling statute. The law in civil law countries is comparable to domestic contract law. Discretionary investment accounts are somewhat similar to managing agency accounts. Specific administrative guidelines and related statutes may exist in some form in civil law countries. Those requirements, and not common law fiduciary standards, are applied in the examination process.
However, the duty of the bank in such circumstances to perform faithfully the terms of its contract with the customer involves a high degree of fiduciary responsibility.
Private holding companies are administered in both common and civil law jurisdictions and are created to provide significant tax benefits and confidentiality. In this type of arrangement, the underlying assets of the holding company are held in nominee name. Shares of the holding company in turn are issued to and reflected on the branch or affiliate's account records. The duties of the bank or its affiliate may involve basic administration of corporate affairs and, depending on the particular arrangement, investment responsibility over the company's underlying assets. The shares of the company may be held in a trust account or under an investment advisory contract, which can result in additional responsibilities. The major types of holding companies include private investment companies, which hold passive assets for investment purposes, and private trading companies, which hold operating assets for reinvoicing and other commercial purposes. Captive insurance companies also may be administered in various capacities. Generally, the capacity must be considered prior to making any determination regarding compliance.
Conflicts of Interest/Self-Dealing The application of the work program for conflicts of interest/self-dealing should be adapted to the context International Fiduciary Activities of both foreign practices and international aspects of domestic activity. Although self-dealing, as known in the U.S., is sometimes a recognized business practice abroad, the basic fiduciary principle against such activity is applicable unless specifically authorized. The application of this work program to the international aspects of domestic fiduciary accounts is based upon 12 CFR 9 and domestic statutes, particularly in the areas of insider trading and the underwriting of securities.
Review of Regulatory Reports The applicable report in all international and some domestic examinations is the Annual Report of International Fiduciary Activities (FFIEC-006), which indicates fiduciary activities in overseas locations. This report and the related responses constitute a call report under the authority of 12 U.S.C. 161 and 12 U.S.C. 1817, and must be filed annually by all national banks having an overseas branch or affiliate.

Federal Branches Fiduciary Activities On September 10, 1984, the OCC first granted approval to exercise full fiduciary powers to a "federal branch" of a foreign bank operating in the United States. Approval was granted under 12 U.S.C. 92a and in accordance with 12 CFR 5. That regulation specifies that an application to exercise fiduciary powers may be submitted by a foreign bank at the time of filing for a federal branch license or at any subsequent date. Generally, federal branches have the same rights and privileges and are subject to the same duties, restrictions, penalties, liabilities, conditions and limitations as national banks doing business at the same location. The OCC also supervises their fiduciary activities in a similar manner.
However, certain areas of federal branch fiduciary activities require particular supervisory attention. They are: Organization and supervision.
Organization and Supervision A federal branch may differ from a traditional fiduciary organization in various ways. Although directorates of banks offering fiduciary products and services usually operate on-site, a federal branch is subject to direction by the board of its parent bank at the home office. Such a directorate is unable to supervise fiduciary activities directly and normally will assign day-to-day fiduciary responsibilities, except for the audit function, to local management and a local supervisory committee.
Another important difference is that most board members, managers and officers may have accumulated much of their experience abroad, particularly in the country of the branch's home office. Such differences may result in unique or unusual characteristics in products, services, bookkeeping, and delivery systems.
Another possible result is full integration of fiduciary products and services with other bank services. Fiduciary activities, no matter how unique or widely dispersed, must be identified and their associated risks determined in order to implement proper supervisory strategies or to perform adequate examinations.
A major responsibility of the board, fulfilled through the branch, is to provide professional investment management services to fiduciary customers. Directors should ensure that assets comprising each managed portfolio are analyzed carefully and selected to fulfill the objectives of the particular account. All assets held in managed accounts must be subject to written reviews initially upon acceptance and periodically thereafter in compliance with 12 CFR 9.7(a)(2).
Audit Audit responsibility for fiduciary activities must be assigned under 12 CFR 9.9 to an audit committee composed of bank directors. Since a federal branch has no directors, a local audit committee could not meet the membership requirements of that regulation. Therefore, an audit committee charged with fiduciary activities should be established at a higher organizational level.
Under any organizational structure, the audit function must be part of a program which meets OCC standards for competence, independence, and coverage.
The examiner must be alert to the possibility that audit procedures for federal branch fiduciary activities are widely dispersed throughout a number of audit programs rather than being consolidated into one program. Nevertheless, those programs and procedures should conform to requirements contained in this handbook. The branch should either provide a complete audit report of fiduciary activities or consolidate, from all audit reports, those comments related to fiduciary activities.
Accounting Records Accounting software, including that developed outside the U.S., must comply with federal statutes and regulations. Examiners must ascertain that federal branches maintain a separate set of books and records for fiduciary activities, as required by 12 U.S.C. 92a(c). Records of U.S. fiduciary organizations differ substantially from those of European banks or brokerage firms. In most European countries, banking is not separated from securities activities, nor is investment management activity segregated from commercial lending as strictly as in the U.S. Bankers and examiners should determine whether federal branch fiduciary records of cash and assets are segregated adequately from other bank records.
Collateral for Fiduciary Funds 12 U.S.C 92a(d) requires that uninsured fiduciary funds awaiting investment or distribution, deposited with the branch, be secured by bonds or other securities. Such deposits take several forms, including time deposits, certificates of deposit, NOW accounts and demand deposit accounts. Most banks deposit a portion of fiduciary funds awaiting investment or distribution in a single large demand deposit account. A seldom used alternative is an individual demand deposit account for each fiduciary account. Inefficiencies, particularly the increased number of periodic reconcilements required for each demand deposit account, have virtually limited such a system to very small operations. Federal branches usually have no FDIC insurance and therefore are distinctly different from national banks that are insured up to $100,000 on each account. Since federal branches are uninsured, all such deposits must be collateralized.
Local law may require the federal branch to post an additional security deposit, usually a set percentage of Federal Branches Fiduciary Activities capital stock, for the benefit of private and court trusts.
Since a federal branch is not incorporated separately and does not have its own "capital," this deposit is considered a "capital" equivalency deposit. The capital equivalency deposit is kept on deposit with a member bank, in such amount as the Comptroller of the Currency specifies, in the form of dollar deposits or investment securities of the type that may be held by national banks for their own account pursuant to 12 U.S.C 24(7).
Examiners must be aware of the existence of state laws that may require that the security deposit be based on capital of the parent bank rather than on the capital equivalency deposit.
Legal Counsel 12 CFR 9.7(c) requires federal branches to "... designate, employ or retain legal counsel who shall be readily available to pass upon fiduciary matters and to advise the bank and its trust department." This is particularly important for the development and operation of a new organization. The legal counsel must ensure that federal branch products, services or organizational structures comply with federal law or regulations. An experienced trust counsel can be particularly helpful when managers and technicians do not have in-depth knowledge of U.S. fiduciary operations.

The State banking authorities may have access to reports of examination made by the Comptroller of the Currency insofar as such reports relate to the trust department of such bank, but nothing in this section shall be construed as authorizing the State banking authorities to examine the books, records, and assets of such bank.
Prohibited operations; separate investment accounts; collateral for certain funds used in conduct of business.
(d) No national bank shall receive in its trust department deposits of current funds subject to check or the deposit of checks, drafts, bills of exchange, or other items for collection or exchange purposes. Funds deposited or held in trust by the bank awaiting investment shall be carried in a separate account and shall not be used by the bank in the conduct of its business unless it shall first set aside in the trust department United States bonds or other securities approved by the Comptroller of the Currency.
Lien and claim upon bank failure.
(e) In the event of the failure of such bank the owners of the funds held in trust for investment shall have a lien on the bonds or other securities so set apart in addition to their claim against the estate of the bank.
Deposits of securities for protection of private or court trusts; execution of and exemption from bond.
(f) Whenever the laws of a State require corporations acting in a fiduciary capacity to deposit securities with the State authorities for the protection of private or court trusts, national banks so acting shall be required to make similar deposits and securities so deposited shall be held for the protection of private or court trusts, as provided by the State law. National banks in such cases shall not be required to execute the bond usually required of individuals if State corporations under similar circumstances are exempt from this requirement.
National banks shall have power to execute such bond when so required by the laws of the State.
Officials' oath or affidavit.
(g) In any case in which the laws of a State require that a corporation acting as trustee, executor, administrator, or in any capacity specified in this section, shall take an oath or make an affidavit, the president, vice president, cashier, or trust officer of such national bank may take the necessary oath or execute the necessary affidavit.
Loans of trust funds to officers and employees prohibited; penalties.
(h) It shall be unlawful for any national banking association to lend any officer, director, or employee any funds held in trust under the powers conferred by this section.
Any officer, director, or employee making such loan, or to whom such loan is made, may be fined not more than $5,000, or imprisoned not more than five years, or may be both fined and imprisoned, in the discretion of the court.
Considerations determinative of grant or denial of applications; minimum capital and surplus for issuance of permit.
(i) In passing upon applications for permission to exercise the powers enumerated in this section, the Comptroller of the Currency may take into consideration the amount of capital and surplus of the applying bank, whether or not such capital and surplus is sufficient under the circumstances of the case, the needs of the community to be served, and any other facts and circumstances that seem to him proper, and may grant or refuse the application accordingly: Provided, That no permit shall be issued to any national banking association having a capital and surplus less than the capital and surplus required by State law of State banks, trust companies, and corporations exercising such powers.
IN M Trust Powers of National Banks Fiduciary Laws and Regulations Surrender of authorization; board resolution; Comptroller certification; activities affected; regulations.
(j) Any national banking association desiring to surrender its right to exercise the powers granted under this section, in order to relieve itself of the necessity of complying with the requirements of this section, or to have returned to it any securities which it may have deposited with the State authorities for the protection of private or court trusts, or for any other purpose, may file with the Comptroller of the Currency a certified copy of a resolution of its board of directors signifying such desire. Upon receipt of such resolution, the Comptroller of the Currency, after satisfying himself that such bank has been relieved in accordance with State law of all duties as trustee, executor, administrator, registrar of stocks and bonds, guardian of estates, assignee, receiver, committee of estates of lunatics or other fiduciary, under court, private or other appointments previously accepted under authority of this section, may, in his discretion, issue to such bank a certificate certifying that such bank is no longer authorized to exercise the powers granted by this section. Upon the issuance of such a certificate by the Comptroller of the Currency, such bank (1) shall no longer be subject to the provisions of this section or the regulations of the Comptroller of the Currency made pursuant thereto, (2) shall be entitled to have returned to it any securities which it may have deposited with the State authorities for the protection of private or court trusts, and (3) shall not exercise thereafter any of the powers granted by this section without first applying for and obtaining a new permit to exercise such powers pursuant to the provisions of this section. The Comptroller of the Currency is authorized and empowered to promulgate such regulations as he may deem necessary to enforce compliance with the provisions of this section and the proper exercise of the powers granted therein. Pub.L. 87-722, §1, Sept. 28, 1962, 76 Stat. 668.
Revocation of trust powers.
(k)(1) In addition to the authority conferred by other law, if, in the opinion of the Comptroller of the Currency, a national banking association is unlawfully or unsoundly exercising, or has unlawfully or unsoundly exercised, or has failed for a period of five consecutive years to exercise, the powers granted by this section or otherwise fails or has failed to comply with the requirements of this section, the Comptroller may issue and serve upon the association a notice of intent to revoke the authority of the association to exercise the powers granted by this section. The notice shall contain a statement of the facts constituting the alleged unlawful or unsound exercise of powers, or failure to exercise powers, or failure to comply, and shall fix a time and place at which a hearing will be held to determine whether an order revoking authority to exercise such powers should issue against the association.
(2) Such hearing shall be conducted in accordance with the provisions of subsection (h) of section 8 of the Federal Deposit Insurance Act (12U.S.C. 1818(h)), and subject to judicial review as provided in such section, and shall be fixed for a date not earlier than thirty days nor later than sixty days after service of such notice unless an earlier or later date is set by the Comptroller at the request of any association so served.
(3) Unless the association so served shall appear at the hearing by a duly authorized representative, it shall be deemed to have consented to the issuance of the revocation order. In the event of such consent, or if upon the record made at any such hearing, the Comptroller shall find that any allegation specified in the notice of charges has been established, the Comptroller may issue and serve upon the association an order prohibiting it from accepting any new or additional trust accounts and revoking authority to exercise any and all powers granted by this section, except that such order shall permit the association to continue to service all previously accepted trust accounts pending their expeditious divestiture or termination.
(4) A revocation order shall become effective not earlier than the expiration of 30 days after service of such order upon the association so served (except in the case of a revocation order issued upon consent, which shall become effective at the time specified therein), and shall remain effective and enforceable, except to such extent as it is stayed, modified, terminated, or set aside by action of the Comptroller or a reviewing court.

Employee Retirement Income Security Act of 1974 Fiduciary Laws and Regulations Act of September 2, 1974, 88 Stat. 829, 29 U.S.C. 1001 Title 1 Protection of Employee Benefit Rights §3- ERISA definitions.
